Before compiling, run the layers one at a time and keep each one’s output. The size of each token’s hidden state grows as layers add their updates:

x = model.model.embed_tokens(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE))
states = [x.to_numpy()[0]]
for layer in model.model.layers:
    x = layer(x)
    states.append(x.to_numpy()[0])
states = np.array(states)  # [layer + 1, token, hidden]
labels = [repr(tokenizer.decode([i])) for i in ids]

fig = go.Figure(
    [
        go.Scatter(
            x=np.arange(31),
            y=np.linalg.norm(states[:, t], axis=-1),
            name=label,
            line_color=viz.SERIES[t],
            mode="lines",
            hovertemplate=label + " after layer %{x}: %{y:.1f}<extra></extra>",
        )
        for t, label in enumerate(labels)
    ]
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Hidden-state norm through the layers (0 is the embedding)",
    height=420,
    xaxis_title="layer",
    yaxis=dict(title="norm (log scale)", type="log", dtick=1),
)
viz.show(fig)

The first token behaves differently. Its state jumps to about 24,000 at layer 12, over a hundred times the other tokens’ (about 120 to 165), and stays there until it drops in the last two layers. Models use the first position as a resting place for attention (Section 1.5), and this large state is part of how they do it.

Every layer writes into the same stream, so you can ask what the model would predict if it stopped early: apply the final norm and lm_head to each layer’s output. For the hidden state \(h_\ell\) after layer \(\ell\), the logit lens reads

\[p_\ell = \mathrm{softmax}\big(E \, \mathrm{RMSNorm}(h_\ell)\big)\]

where \(E\) is the embedding table that lm_head shares. Applied to the last position, it shows the prediction forming. Hover to see each layer’s top token:

final_norm = weights["model.norm.weight"]
table = weights["model.embed_tokens.weight"]  # lm_head shares it
paris = tokenizer([" Paris"])["input_ids"][0][0]
last = states[:, -1]
normed = (
    last
    / np.sqrt((last**2).mean(-1, keepdims=True) + config.rms_norm_eps)
    * final_norm
)
lens = normed @ table.T
lens_probs = np.exp(lens - lens.max(-1, keepdims=True))
lens_probs /= lens_probs.sum(-1, keepdims=True)
top_tokens = [repr(tokenizer.decode([int(i)])) for i in lens.argmax(-1)]

fig = go.Figure(
    go.Scatter(
        x=np.arange(31),
        y=lens_probs[:, paris],
        mode="lines+markers",
        line_color=viz.BLUE,
        customdata=top_tokens,
        name="P(' Paris')",
        hovertemplate="layer %{x}: P(' Paris') = %{y:.2f}<br>top token %{customdata}<extra></extra>",
    )
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Logit lens: the probability of ' Paris' after each layer",
    height=400,
    xaxis_title="layer",
    yaxis=dict(title="probability", range=[0, 1]),
)
viz.show(fig)

Through most of the stack the top token is filler: a period or a space, then “ the“ from layer 23. “ Paris“ becomes the top token only after layer 30, the last. Its probability stays near zero through layer 28, reaches about 0.06 after layer 29 and about 0.47 after layer 30, so for this prompt the answer forms in the last two layers.

Before these plots, the model printed 273 parameter tensors and 162.8M values. That is 28M more than the checkpoint’s 134.5M because lm_head counts as its own 49152 × 576 parameter, loaded from the same array as the embedding table. load_state_dict is strict: a missing or misnamed weight raises an error instead of silently keeping a random value, so a successful load already proves the names line up.

Compile

Running 30 layers eagerly works but executes hundreds of operations one at a time. Compiling turns the whole forward pass into one optimized graph. The input is a batch of token-ID sequences of any length:

start = time.perf_counter()
compiled = model.compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
)
print(f"compiled in {time.perf_counter() - start:.0f} s")

start = time.perf_counter()
logits = compiled(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()
print(
    f"forward pass: {(time.perf_counter() - start) * 1000:.0f} ms, logits {logits.shape}"
)

compiled in 10 s
forward pass: 30 ms, logits (1, 5, 49152)

Check against MAX’s SmolLM2

AutoModel loads SmolLM2 with MAX’s own implementation of the architecture that config.json names LlamaForCausalLM, and can return the logits for each token it generates. The first generated token’s logits must match your model’s logits at the last prompt position:

reference_model = AutoModel(MODEL_ID, quantization_encoding="float32")
reference = reference_model.generate(
    input_ids=[ids], max_new_tokens=1, output_logits=True
)
expected = reference.logits[0][0]
reference_model.close()

ours = logits[0, -1]
print(
    f"max difference {np.abs(ours - expected).max():.1e} (logits range up to {np.abs(expected).max():.0f})"
)
print("top token, yours:", repr(tokenizer.decode([int(ours.argmax())])))
print("top token, MAX's:", repr(tokenizer.decode([int(expected.argmax())])))
assert (
    ours.argmax() == expected.argmax() and np.abs(ours - expected).max() < 1e-2
)

max difference 4.0e-05 (logits range up to 21)
top token, yours: ' Paris'
top token, MAX's: ' Paris'

Plot your logits against MAX’s for the 2,000 likeliest tokens. The points sit on the diagonal:

likeliest = np.argsort(expected)[::-1][:2000]
words = [repr(tokenizer.decode([int(i)])) for i in likeliest]
fig = go.Figure(
    go.Scatter(
        x=expected[likeliest],
        y=ours[likeliest],
        mode="markers",
        text=words,
        marker=dict(color=viz.BLUE, size=6, opacity=0.6),
        hovertemplate="%{text}<br>MAX %{x:.4f}<br>yours %{y:.4f}<extra></extra>",
    )
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Next-token logits: your model against MAX's",
    height=480,
    xaxis_title="MAX's logit",
    yaxis=dict(title="your logit", scaleanchor="x"),
)
viz.show(fig)

In the run shown here, on an Apple-silicon Mac with a Metal GPU, the largest difference between your logits and MAX’s was about 4e-5, and both models predict the same next token. Treat these numbers as an example: yours depend on your hardware and vary from run to run. An error in any layer, such as a wrong RoPE layout or a missing scale, would change the logits by whole units, so the match shows that every layer you wrote is correct.

The same code runs the larger SmolLM2 models by changing MODEL_ID. Other Llama-family models need small additions: Llama 3.1, for example, rescales RoPE’s frequencies (rope_scaling in its config), and Qwen2 adds biases to the query, key, and value projections. The handbook’s Choosing the right model discusses how size, architecture, and license trade off when you pick one.

Exercises

  1. The logit lens above reads only the last position. Plot it for every position: at which layer does each one settle on its final prediction?
  2. Load HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M-Instruct with the same code. What changes in config, and how do compile and forward times scale?
  3. Break something on purpose, such as dropping rotate_half from apply_rope, and see how far the logits move.

Next

Section 1.7 turns logits into text: the generation loop, and the sampling strategies behind temperature, top_k, and top_p.

1.7 Generation and sampling

Your model turns tokens into next-token logits. This section turns logits into text: the generation loop, and the sampling strategies behind the temperature, top_k, and top_p settings from Section 1.1. Everything runs on your own model from Section 1.6.

import logging
import time
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from plotly.subplots import make_subplots
from max import functional as F
from max.driver import CPU
from max.dtype import DType
from max.functional import random
from max.pipelines import AutoModel, AutoTokenizer
from max.tensor import Tensor
from max.tensor.tensor import TensorType

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)
model = load_model(config, load_checkpoint(), DEVICE)
compiled = model.compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
)

The generation loop needs one thing from the model: the logits for the next token, given the tokens so far.

def next_logits(ids: list[int]) -> Tensor:
    logits = compiled(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE))
    return logits[0, -1]  # [vocab_size]

Greedy decoding

The simplest strategy picks the most likely token every time:

prompt = tokenizer(["The capital of France is"])["input_ids"][0]
ids = list(prompt)
for _ in range(24):
    ids.append(int(F.argmax(next_logits(ids)).item()))
print(tokenizer.decode(ids))

The capital of France is Paris. Paris is the largest city in France and the capital of the French department of the Espace de Paris.

Greedy decoding is deterministic, so it is the one strategy you can compare token for token with MAX’s own pipeline. top_k=1 makes AutoModel greedy too:

reference = AutoModel(MODEL_ID, quantization_encoding="float32")
expected = reference.generate(
    input_ids=[prompt], max_new_tokens=24, top_k=1
).sequences[0]
reference.close()
print("same tokens as AutoModel:", list(expected[-24:]) == ids[-24:])
assert list(expected[-24:]) == ids[-24:]

same tokens as AutoModel: True

Greedy text is coherent but tends to repeat itself and to pick the blandest continuation. Sampling picks tokens at random, in proportion to their probability, to get more varied text.

Temperature

Softmax turns logits \(z\) into probabilities. Dividing the logits by a temperature \(T\) first reshapes the distribution:

\[p_i = \frac{e^{z_i / T}}{\sum_j e^{z_j / T}}\]

Below 1, the likely tokens get likelier; above 1, the distribution flattens toward uniform. As \(T \to 0\), sampling becomes greedy. Move the slider to see it on the twelve likeliest next tokens of a story:

story = tokenizer(["Once upon a time, there was a"])["input_ids"][0]
logits = next_logits(story)
top = np.argsort(logits.to_numpy())[::-1][:12]
labels = [repr(tokenizer.decode([int(i)])) for i in top]

temperatures = [0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0, 1.25, 1.5, 2.0]
steps = []
for temperature in temperatures:
    probs = F.softmax(logits / temperature).to_numpy()
    steps.append(
        [
            go.Bar(
                x=labels,
                y=probs[top],
                marker_color=viz.BLUE,
                hovertemplate="%{x}: %{y:.3f}<extra></extra>",
            )
        ]
    )
fig = go.Figure(
    layout=dict(
        title="Next-token probabilities after 'Once upon a time, there was a'",
        yaxis=dict(title="probability", range=[0, 1]),
        height=460,
        bargap=0.25,
    )
)
viz.show(
    viz.with_slider(
        fig, steps, [str(t) for t in temperatures], "temperature ", active=4
    )
)

Sampling with MAX operations

To sample from a distribution, you could compute the softmax and its cumulative sum, then search it with a random number. The Gumbel-max trick does it with operations you already have: add Gumbel noise \(-\log(-\log u)\), with \(u\) uniform in \((0, 1)\), to the scaled logits, and take the argmax:

\[\text{token} = \arg\max_i \left( \frac{z_i}{T} - \log(-\log u_i) \right), \qquad u_i \sim \mathrm{Uniform}(0, 1)\]

The sampled tokens follow the softmax distribution exactly, without computing the softmax. A quick check against the probabilities:

def gumbel_argmax(scores: Tensor) -> Tensor:
    u = random.uniform(
        scores.shape,
        range=(1e-6, 1.0),
        dtype=scores.dtype,
        device=scores.device,
    )
    return F.argmax(scores - F.log(-F.log(u)))


toy = Tensor(np.array([2.0, 1.0, 0.5, 0.1, -1.0], np.float32), device=DEVICE)
counts = np.bincount(
    [int(gumbel_argmax(toy).item()) for _ in range(2000)], minlength=5
)
print("sampled:", (counts / 2000).round(3))
print("softmax:", F.softmax(toy).to_numpy().round(3))
assert np.abs(counts / 2000 - F.softmax(toy).to_numpy()).max() < 0.05

sampled: [0.556 0.205 0.133 0.08  0.026]
softmax: [0.559 0.205 0.125 0.084 0.028]

Top-k and top-p

Even at a sensible temperature, the long tail of unlikely tokens adds up, and one bad draw can derail the text. Two filters cut the tail before sampling:

  • Top-k keeps the \(k\) most likely tokens.
  • Top-p (nucleus sampling) keeps the smallest set of tokens whose probabilities add up to at least \(p\), so it keeps few tokens when the model is confident and many when it isn’t.

Both filters work on tokens sorted from most to least likely. F.top_k returns the \(k\) largest scores already sorted, with their token IDs. For top-p alone, F.argsort sorts the whole vocabulary and F.gather puts the scores in that order. F.cumsum over the sorted probabilities then gives the running total, and F.where drops every token that the total had already passed \(p\) before reaching. The filtered scores go through the Gumbel-max trick, and the ID map turns the winning position back into a token. Without a filter, nothing needs sorting.

from __future__ import annotations

from collections.abc import Callable

from max import functional as F
from max.functional import random
from max.tensor import Tensor


def sample(
    logits: Tensor,
    temperature: float = 1.0,
    top_k: int | None = None,
    top_p: float | None = None,
) -> int:
    """Picks a token ID from 1-D ``logits``; ``temperature=0`` is greedy."""
    if temperature == 0:
        return int(F.argmax(logits).item())
    scores, token_ids = logits / temperature, None
    if top_k is not None:
        scores, token_ids = F.top_k(scores, top_k)  # sorted, largest first
    if top_p is not None:
        if token_ids is None:
            token_ids = F.argsort(scores, ascending=False)
            scores = F.gather(scores, token_ids, axis=0)
        probs = F.softmax(scores)
        before = F.cumsum(probs, axis=-1) - probs  # mass of likelier tokens
        scores = F.where(before < top_p, scores, float("-inf"))
    u = random.uniform(
        scores.shape,
        range=(1e-6, 1.0),
        dtype=scores.dtype,
        device=scores.device,
    )
    choice = int(F.argmax(scores - F.log(-F.log(u))).item())
    return choice if token_ids is None else int(token_ids.to_numpy()[choice])


def generate(
    next_logits: Callable[[list[int]], Tensor],
    prompt: list[int],
    max_new_tokens: int,
    eos_token_id: int | None = None,
    **sampling: float | None,
) -> list[int]:
    """Generates up to ``max_new_tokens`` tokens after ``prompt``.

    ``next_logits`` maps the tokens so far to the next token's logits.
    Generation stops early at ``eos_token_id``. Returns only the new tokens.
    """
    ids = list(prompt)
    for _ in range(max_new_tokens):
        token = sample(next_logits(ids), **sampling)
        ids.append(token)
        if token == eos_token_id:
            break
    return ids[len(prompt) :]

Saved to minillm/sampling.py.

Top-p adapts to the model’s confidence. After “The capital of France is”, two tokens hold about three quarters of the probability; after “Once upon a time, there was a”, it spreads over many. Move \(p\) and compare how many tokens each prompt keeps (blue) and cuts (gray). The bars show the likeliest 40; the count covers the whole vocabulary:

prompts = ["The capital of France is", "Once upon a time, there was a"]
shown = 40
sorted_probs = []
for text in prompts:
    probs = F.softmax(next_logits(tokenizer([text])["input_ids"][0])).to_numpy()
    sorted_probs.append(np.sort(probs)[::-1])

ps = [0.5, 0.75, 0.9, 0.95, 0.99]
rank = np.arange(1, shown + 1)
steps = []
for p in ps:
    group = []
    for axis, probs in zip(["", "2"], sorted_probs):
        before = np.cumsum(probs) - probs  # the same test as `sample`
        kept = before < p
        count = int(kept.sum())
        kept, top = kept[:shown], probs[:shown]
        for mask, color, name in [
            (kept, viz.BLUE, "kept"),
            (~kept, viz.MUTED, "cut"),
        ]:
            group.append(
                go.Bar(
                    x=rank[mask],
                    y=top[mask],
                    marker_color=color,
                    name=name,
                    showlegend=False,
                    xaxis=f"x{axis}",
                    yaxis=f"y{axis}",
                    hovertemplate="rank %{x}: %{y:.3f}<extra>"
                    + name
                    + "</extra>",
                )
            )
        group.append(
            go.Scatter(
                x=[shown],
                y=[0.9],
                mode="text",
                text=[f"{count:,} kept"],
                textposition="middle left",
                showlegend=False,
                hoverinfo="skip",
                xaxis=f"x{axis}",
                yaxis=f"y{axis}",
            )
        )
    steps.append(group)

fig = make_subplots(
    rows=2,
    cols=1,
    shared_xaxes=True,
    vertical_spacing=0.12,
    subplot_titles=[repr(text) for text in prompts],
)
fig.update_layout(
    height=560,
    bargap=0.15,
    title="The likeliest 40 next tokens, sorted, at temperature 1",
)
fig.update_yaxes(range=[0, 1], title="probability")
fig.update_xaxes(title="rank", row=2, col=1)
viz.show(viz.with_slider(fig, steps, [str(p) for p in ps], "top_p ", active=2))

generate stops at the model’s end-of-sequence token, which a chat model emits when its answer is complete. Try a few settings. random.set_seed makes a sampled run repeatable:

for settings in [
    dict(temperature=0),
    dict(temperature=0.7, top_p=0.9),
    dict(temperature=1.0, top_k=50),
    dict(temperature=1.5),
]:
    random.set_seed(0)
    new = generate(next_logits, story, 24, config.eos_token_id, **settings)
    print(f"{settings}\n  {tokenizer.decode(new)!r}\n")

{'temperature': 0}
  ' young woman named Sarah who was struggling to find her place in the world. She had always been a bit of a rebel'

{'temperature': 0.7, 'top_p': 0.9}
  ' young girl named Lily who was determined to be a better version of herself. She had always been the "perfect" girl'

{'temperature': 1.0, 'top_k': 50}
  ' young musician who had spent their life playing and composing. He was known as "The Weaver" due to his exceptional skill'

{'temperature': 1.5}
  ' neighboring realm on your planet, mist. Even then; Unlocking beastellandsis deathshire istsubbralgenaki'

Higher temperatures read less predictably. At 1.5 without a filter, the text turns to gibberish within a few words. Many chat applications use settings near temperature=0.7 and top_p=0.9. The handbook’s inference parameters page covers these and the other settings servers accept, such as stop sequences and repetition penalties.

The cost of each token

Generating the token after \(n\) tokens runs the model over all \(n\) of them. Time that step at several lengths, with random token IDs since only the length matters:

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
lengths = [16, 64, 256, 512, 1024, 2048]
step_ms = []
for length in lengths:
    ids = rng.integers(0, config.vocab_size, length).tolist()
    next_logits(ids).to_numpy()  # warm up this length
    times = []
    for _ in range(3):
        start = time.perf_counter()
        next_logits(ids).to_numpy()
        times.append((time.perf_counter() - start) * 1000)
    step_ms.append(min(times))
    print(f"{length:5} tokens: {step_ms[-1]:6.1f} ms per new token")

fig = go.Figure(
    go.Scatter(
        x=lengths,
        y=step_ms,
        mode="lines+markers",
        line_color=viz.ORANGE,
        hovertemplate="%{x} tokens: %{y:.1f} ms<extra></extra>",
    )
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Time to generate one token, recomputing the whole sequence",
    xaxis_title="sequence length (tokens)",
    yaxis_title="ms per token",
    yaxis_rangemode="tozero",
    height=380,
)
viz.show(fig)

   16 tokens:   82.2 ms per new token
   64 tokens:  104.0 ms per new token
  256 tokens:  101.7 ms per new token
  512 tokens:  186.9 ms per new token
 1024 tokens:  570.1 ms per new token
 2048 tokens: 1906.7 ms per new token

In the run shown here, on an Apple-silicon Mac with a Metal GPU, a step took about 80 to 105 ms up to 256 tokens, about 570 ms at 1,024 tokens, and about 1.9 s at 2,048. Treat these numbers as an example: yours depend on your hardware and vary from run to run. For short sequences the step costs about the same at any length, because fixed work, such as reading every weight and launching each kernel, dominates. Past a few hundred tokens the cost climbs faster than the length itself, because attention compares every position with every earlier one. Other devices give other numbers with the same shape.

The keys and values of earlier positions never change, so all the work of recomputing them is wasted. Section 1.8 caches them. The handbook’s LLM inference metrics page explains why this per-token time, the TPOT from Section 1.1, is the number users feel while a reply streams in.

Exercises

  1. Sample the same prompt 5 times at temperature=1.0 with and without top_p=0.9. How often does each run go off topic?
  2. Implement min-p sampling: keep tokens whose probability is at least min_p times the top token’s probability. Which MAX operations do you need?
  3. Plot how many tokens top-p keeps at each step of a generation. When is the model confident?

Next

Section 1.8 adds a KV cache so each step processes one token instead of the whole sequence.

1.8 The KV cache

Section 1.7 ended with a problem: each generated token reruns the model over the whole sequence, so the cost per token grows with the text. This section fixes it the way inference engines do, with a KV cache, and measures the difference.

Why caching works

To compute attention for a new token, the model needs the keys and values of every earlier position, in every layer. Those keys and values depend only on the tokens up to their own position, so they never change once computed. Recomputing them every step is wasted work. The KV cache stores them instead, which splits generation into two phases:

  • Prefill runs the whole prompt at once, like before, and saves every layer’s keys and values.
  • Decode runs one new token per step. Each layer computes the new token’s query, key, and value, appends the key and value to its cache, and attends over the whole cache.

The handbook’s How does LLM inference work? page describes the same two phases from the serving side.

import logging
import time
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from max.driver import CPU
from plotly.subplots import make_subplots
from max.dtype import DType
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer
from max.tensor import Tensor
from max.tensor.tensor import TensorType

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.sampling import generate
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
weights = load_checkpoint()
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)

The difference in work is easy to see. Each row below is one generation step for a 6-token prompt; each column is a position whose key and value that step computes. Without a cache, every step recomputes every earlier position. With one, prefill computes the prompt once and each decode step computes one new position:

prompt_len, steps = 6, 10
step_labels = ["prefill"] + [f"decode {i}" for i in range(1, steps)]
recompute_work = np.zeros((steps, prompt_len + steps - 1))
cached_work = np.zeros_like(recompute_work)
for step in range(steps):
    end = prompt_len + step
    recompute_work[step, :end] = 1
    cached_work[step, :end] = 1 if step == 0 else 0
    cached_work[step, end - 1] = 1

fig = make_subplots(
    rows=1,
    cols=2,
    shared_yaxes=True,
    horizontal_spacing=0.06,
    subplot_titles=[
        f"Recompute: {int(recompute_work.sum())} key/value computations",
        f"KV cache: {int(cached_work.sum())} key/value computations",
    ],
)
for col, work in enumerate([recompute_work, cached_work], start=1):
    fig.add_trace(
        go.Heatmap(
            z=np.where(work > 0, 1, np.nan),
            y=step_labels,
            xgap=2,
            ygap=2,
            colorscale=[[0, viz.BLUE], [1, viz.BLUE]],
            showscale=False,
            hovertemplate="%{y}: computes position %{x}<extra></extra>",
        ),
        row=1,
        col=col,
    )
fig.update_xaxes(title="position", showgrid=False, dtick=2)
fig.update_yaxes(autorange="reversed", showgrid=False)
fig.update_layout(height=380, title="Which keys and values each step computes")
viz.show(fig)

How big is the cache?

Each token stores one key and one value vector per key/value head, per layer. With \(L\) layers, \(H_{kv}\) key/value heads of dimension \(d\), and \(b\) bytes per number:

\[\text{bytes per token} = 2 \cdot L \cdot H_{kv} \cdot d \cdot b = 2 \cdot 30 \cdot 3 \cdot 64 \cdot 4\]

per_token = (
    2
    * config.num_hidden_layers
    * config.num_key_value_heads
    * config.head_dim
    * 4
)
print(f"{per_token / 1024:.0f} KiB per token in float32")
print(
    f"{per_token * 8192 / 2**20:.0f} MiB for SmolLM2's full 8192-token context"
)
print(
    f"{per_token * 3 * 8192 / 2**20:.0f} MiB if every query head had its own key/value head"
)

45 KiB per token in float32
360 MiB for SmolLM2's full 8192-token context
1080 MiB if every query head had its own key/value head

The cache grows with the context and with the number of sequences served at once. Move the slider to serve more sequences, and compare the cache with the model’s weights:

lengths = np.arange(256, 8193, 256)
weights_mib = sum(w.nbytes for w in weights.values()) / 2**20
batches = [1, 4, 16, 64]
steps = []
for batch in batches:
    steps.append(
        [
            go.Scatter(
                x=lengths,
                y=per_token * lengths * batch / 2**20,
                name="KV cache, 3 key/value heads",
                line_color=viz.BLUE,
                hovertemplate="%{x} tokens: %{y:,.0f} MiB<extra></extra>",
            ),
            go.Scatter(
                x=lengths,
                y=3 * per_token * lengths * batch / 2**20,
                name="KV cache, 9 key/value heads",
                line=dict(color=viz.ORANGE, dash="dot"),
                hovertemplate="%{x} tokens: %{y:,.0f} MiB<extra></extra>",
            ),
        ]
    )
fig = go.Figure(
    go.Scatter(
        x=[lengths[0], lengths[-1]],
        y=[weights_mib] * 2,
        name="weights",
        mode="lines",
        line=dict(color=viz.INK_SECONDARY, dash="dash"),
        hovertemplate="weights: %{y:,.0f} MiB<extra></extra>",
    )
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="float32 KV cache memory against context length",
    height=460,
    xaxis_title="context length (tokens)",
    yaxis=dict(title="MiB", type="log", dtick=1),
)
viz.show(
    viz.with_slider(
        fig, steps, [str(b) for b in batches], "sequences served at once: "
    )
)

This is where grouped-query attention (Section 1.5) pays off: 3 key/value heads instead of 9 make the cache 3 times smaller. Even for SmolLM2 in float32, two sequences at the full 8,192-token context need a 720 MiB cache, more than the 513 MiB of weights. For large models serving many users, the cache often takes more GPU memory than the weights. The handbook’s GPU memory calculation adds it to the weights when sizing a deployment.

Attention with a cache

CachedAttention extends Section 1.5’s Attention. Its forward takes the layer’s cached keys and values, if any, and returns them extended by the new positions. start is the position of the first new token, so RoPE rotates the new queries and keys for where they really are.

Prefill uses the causal mask as before. Decode has one query, the newest token, which may attend to every cached position, so it needs no mask.

from __future__ import annotations

import math

import numpy as np
from max import functional as F
from max.driver import Device
from max.dtype import DType
from max.nn import ModuleList
from max.nn.kernels.attention import flash_attention
from max.tensor import Tensor
from max.tensor.tensor import TensorType

from minillm.attention import Attention
from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import DecoderLayer, LlamaForCausalLM, LlamaModel

KVPair = tuple[Tensor, Tensor]


class CachedAttention(Attention):
    """Attention that reads and extends a key/value cache."""

    def forward(
        self, x: Tensor, start: Tensor, past: KVPair | None = None
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, KVPair]:
        q, k, v = self.project(x, start)
        if past is None:  # prefill: causal attention over the prompt
            return self.attend(q, k, v), (k, v)
        k = F.concat([past[0], k], axis=1)
        v = F.concat([past[1], v], axis=1)
        # The one new token may attend to every cached position: no mask.
        out = flash_attention(q, k, v, 1 / math.sqrt(self.head_dim))
        out = out.rebind_mapping(q.mapping)
        batch, seq = q.shape[0], q.shape[1]
        out = out.reshape([batch, seq, self.num_heads * self.head_dim])
        return self.o_proj(out), (k, v)

Saved to minillm/kv_cache.py.

The layer and model pass each layer’s cache through. The model returns logits for the last position only: generation never needs the others, and skipping them saves most of the LM head’s work during prefill.

class CachedDecoderLayer(DecoderLayer):
    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.self_attn = CachedAttention(config)

    def forward(
        self, x: Tensor, start: Tensor, past: KVPair | None = None
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, KVPair]:
        attn, kv = self.self_attn(self.input_layernorm(x), start, past)
        x = x + attn
        return x + self.mlp(self.post_attention_layernorm(x)), kv


class CachedLlamaModel(LlamaModel):
    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.layers = ModuleList(
            [
                CachedDecoderLayer(config)
                for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)
            ]
        )

    def forward(
        self,
        input_ids: Tensor,
        start: Tensor,
        cache: list[KVPair] | None = None,
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, list[KVPair]]:
        x = self.embed_tokens(input_ids)
        new_cache = []
        for i, layer in enumerate(self.layers):
            x, kv = layer(x, start, None if cache is None else cache[i])
            new_cache.append(kv)
        return self.norm(x), new_cache


class CachedLlamaForCausalLM(LlamaForCausalLM):
    """Returns the last position's logits and the updated cache."""

    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.model = CachedLlamaModel(config)

    def forward(
        self,
        input_ids: Tensor,
        start: Tensor,
        cache: list[KVPair] | None = None,
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, list[KVPair]]:
        hidden, new_cache = self.model(input_ids, start, cache)
        return self.lm_head(hidden[:, -1]), new_cache

Saved to minillm/kv_cache.py.

Two compiled graphs

Prefill and decode have different inputs, so they compile into two graphs that share the same weights. The cache is a list of 30 (key, value) pairs. Its length, cache_len, is a named dimension, so one decode graph serves every step. start is a one-element tensor rather than a Python number, so that it is an input of the graph instead of a constant baked into it.

KVCacheDecoder keeps the cache between calls. It has the same interface as Section 1.7’s next_logits: given the tokens so far, it returns the next token’s logits, running a prefill for a new prompt and a decode step when exactly one token was added. It plugs straight into generate.

class KVCacheDecoder:
    """Returns next-token logits like a full forward pass, using a cache."""

    def __init__(
        self, model: CachedLlamaForCausalLM, config: LlamaConfig, device: Device
    ) -> None:
        self.device = device
        tokens = TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=device)
        one_token = TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", 1), device=device)
        start = TensorType(DType.float32, [1], device=device)
        kv = TensorType(
            DType.float32,
            ("batch", "cache_len", config.num_key_value_heads, config.head_dim),
            device=device,
        )
        self.prefill = model.compile(tokens, start)
        self.decode = model.compile(
            one_token, start, [(kv, kv)] * config.num_hidden_layers
        )
        self.cache: list[KVPair] | None = None
        self.length = 0

    def __call__(self, ids: list[int]) -> Tensor:
        if self.cache is not None and len(ids) == self.length + 1:
            logits, self.cache = self.decode(
                self._tokens(ids[-1:]), self._position(self.length), self.cache
            )
        else:
            logits, self.cache = self.prefill(
                self._tokens(ids), self._position(0)
            )
        self.length = len(ids)
        return logits[0]

    def _tokens(self, ids: list[int]) -> Tensor:
        return Tensor(np.array([ids], dtype=np.int64), device=self.device)

    def _position(self, position: int) -> Tensor:
        return Tensor(
            np.array([position], dtype=np.float32), device=self.device
        )

Saved to minillm/kv_cache.py.

start = time.perf_counter()
cached = KVCacheDecoder(
    load_model(config, weights, DEVICE, CachedLlamaForCausalLM), config, DEVICE
)
print(f"compiled prefill and decode in {time.perf_counter() - start:.0f} s")

compiled prefill and decode in 24 s

Check it

The cache must not change the result. Generate greedily with Section 1.7’s full recompute and with the cache, and compare the tokens:

full = load_model(config, weights, DEVICE).compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
)


def recompute(ids: list[int]) -> Tensor:
    return full(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE))[0, -1]


prompt = tokenizer(["The capital of France is"])["input_ids"][0]
slow = generate(recompute, prompt, 48, temperature=0)
fast = generate(cached, prompt, 48, temperature=0)
print("identical tokens:", slow == fast)
assert slow == fast
print(tokenizer.decode(prompt + fast))
print("cached keys per layer:", [int(d) for d in cached.cache[0][0].shape])

identical tokens: True
The capital of France is Paris. Paris is the largest city in France and the capital of the French department of the Espace de Paris.

The Eiffel Tower, also known as the Eiffel Tower, is a monumental structure that stands 324
cached keys per layer: [1, 52, 3, 64]

Measure it

Time one decode step at several context lengths, with random prompts so that only the length matters:

def ms_per_token(next_logits, prompt: list[int], steps: int = 24) -> float:
    ids = list(prompt)
    ids.append(int(np.argmax(next_logits(ids).to_numpy())))  # prefill, untimed
    start = time.perf_counter()
    for _ in range(steps):
        ids.append(int(np.argmax(next_logits(ids).to_numpy())))
    return (time.perf_counter() - start) / steps * 1000


rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
lengths = [32, 128, 256, 512, 1024]
timings = {"recompute": [], "KV cache": []}
for length in lengths:
    prompt = rng.integers(0, config.vocab_size, length).tolist()
    timings["recompute"].append(ms_per_token(recompute, prompt))
    timings["KV cache"].append(ms_per_token(cached, prompt))

fig = go.Figure(
    [
        go.Scatter(
            x=lengths,
            y=ms,
            name=name,
            mode="lines+markers",
            line_color=color,
            hovertemplate=name + ", %{x} tokens: %{y:.1f} ms<extra></extra>",
        )
        for (name, ms), color in zip(timings.items(), [viz.ORANGE, viz.BLUE])
    ]
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Time per decode step",
    height=420,
    xaxis_title="context length (tokens)",
    yaxis_title="ms per token",
    yaxis_rangemode="tozero",
)
viz.show(fig)

Hover over the points to read the timings. On this page’s machine, an Apple-silicon Mac with a Metal GPU, a cached decode step took about 130 to 160 ms at every length, while recompute grew from about 150 ms at 32 tokens to about 610 ms at 1,024. Treat these numbers as an example: yours depend on your hardware and vary from run to run. At short contexts the two take about the same time, because fixed work, such as launching kernels and reading every weight, dominates a step of a model this small. The cached step still does more work as the context grows: attention reads a longer cache, and F.concat copies the whole cache into a new tensor every step. At these lengths that growth is smaller than the variation between runs.

This simple cache has limits that production engines address:

  • Memory is allocated per sequence, as it grows. A server doesn’t know how long each reply will be, and contiguous per-sequence buffers fragment memory. Paged attention stores the cache in fixed-size blocks instead, like virtual memory pages.
  • It serves one sequence at a time. A decode step for one token barely uses the hardware. Engines batch many sequences per step, which needs a cache that holds sequences of different lengths.
  • Shared prompts are recomputed. Requests that start with the same system prompt could share those cache blocks (prefix caching).

Chapter 5 builds these into a small serving engine: Section 5.2 adds continuous batching, Section 5.3 a paged KV cache, and Section 5.4 prefix caching. The handbook covers each one: PagedAttention, batching, and prefix caching.

Exercises

  1. Extend the timing chart to context lengths from 16 to 2048. Below what length does the cache stop paying off on your device, and why?
  2. Make KVCacheDecoder keep the cache across two turns of a conversation: prefill only the new turn’s tokens after the first reply.
  3. Store the cache in bfloat16 instead of float32. How much memory does it save, and do the greedy tokens change?

What you built

Chapter 1 is complete. You have a working LLM inference stack written with MAX: a tokenizer and weight loader, a Llama-architecture model that matches MAX’s own, sampling, and a KV cache. Chapter 2 splits this model across several devices with MAX’s distributed tensors.

2. Distributed tensors

Large models don’t fit on one device, and even small ones serve faster when several devices share the work. In MAX, a tensor that spans several devices behaves like any other tensor: you describe how it is laid out, and MAX runs each operation on every device and inserts the communication it needs.

Distributed code is hard to develop when every test needs a multi-GPU machine. MAX can simulate a mesh of devices on one CPU, with the same code and the same results, so this whole chapter runs on a laptop or in Colab.

SectionYou learn
2.1 Distributed tensorsDevice meshes, placements, sharded matmuls, and the collectives they imply.
2.2 A tensor-parallel modelSplitting SmolLM2 across three devices and checking that its logits don’t change.

The handbook’s parallelism strategies and distributed inference pages explain when each strategy pays off in production.

2.1 Distributed tensors

A distributed tensor is one logical array whose data lives on several devices. In MAX it is an ordinary Tensor, the same type you used from Section 1.2 on: operators, max.functional, and max.nn layers accept it, run on every device that holds a piece, and add the communication between devices that the math requires.

This section runs everything on a simulated mesh: four “devices” that are all the same CPU. The program, the per-device pieces, and the results are the same as on four GPUs, so you can develop and test distributed code on a laptop and run it unchanged on real hardware.

import logging
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from max import functional as F
from max.driver import CPU
from max.tensor import Tensor
from max.tensor.sharding import (
    DeviceMapping,
    DeviceMesh,
    Partial,
    Replicated,
    Sharded,
    auto_reshard,
    visualize,
)
from plotly.subplots import make_subplots

from minillm import viz

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)

Meshes and placements

A DeviceMesh arranges devices on named axes. This one has four devices on one axis, named tp for tensor parallelism:

mesh = DeviceMesh((CPU(),) * 4, (4,), ("tp",))


def on(*placements) -> DeviceMapping:
    """Places a tensor on the mesh, with one placement per mesh axis."""
    return DeviceMapping(mesh, placements)

A DeviceMapping says how a tensor’s data relates to the devices on each mesh axis. That relation is its placement:

  • Replicated(): every device holds the whole tensor.
  • Sharded(axis): the tensor is split along axis, one piece per device.
  • Partial(): every device holds a full-size tensor, and the real value is their sum.

Split an 8 × 4 matrix by rows:

a = np.arange(32, dtype=np.float32).reshape(8, 4)
x = Tensor(a, device=on(Sharded(0)))
visualize(x)

DType.float32 [8, 4] with DeviceMapping(DeviceMesh(tp=4), [Sharded(axis=0)])
┌──────────┐   tensor[:, :]
│ device 0 │   ├── tp=0  device 0 - tensor[:2, :]
│ [:2, :]  │   ├── tp=1  device 1 - tensor[2:4, :]
├──────────┤   ├── tp=2  device 2 - tensor[4:6, :]
│ device 1 │   └── tp=3  device 3 - tensor[6:, :]
│ [2:4, :] │
├──────────┤
│ device 2 │
│ [4:6, :] │
├──────────┤
│ device 3 │
│ [6:, :]  │
└──────────┘

x.shape is the global shape, [8, 4], as if the tensor lived on one device. Each device’s piece, its shard, is in local_shards:

print("global shape:", [int(d) for d in x.shape])
for i, shard in enumerate(x.local_shards):
    print(f"device {i}:", [int(d) for d in shard.shape], shard.to_numpy()[:, 0])

global shape: [8, 4]
device 0: [2, 4] [0. 4.]
device 1: [2, 4] [ 8. 12.]
device 2: [2, 4] [16. 20.]
device 3: [2, 4] [24. 28.]

The next helper turns a tensor’s placement into a map of which device holds each element, using only mapping.placements and the shard shapes. The figures in this section draw it: one color per device, gray where every device holds the element, and a darker gray for a Partial tensor, whose every device holds a term of the sum.

ALL, PARTIAL = -1, -2


def owners(t: Tensor) -> np.ndarray:
    """Returns the device that holds each element of ``t`` on a 1-D mesh."""
    grid = np.full([int(d) for d in t.shape], ALL)
    placement = t.mapping.placements[0]
    if isinstance(placement, Partial):
        grid[:] = PARTIAL
    elif isinstance(placement, Sharded):
        start = 0
        for device, shard in enumerate(t.local_shards):
            size = int(shard.shape[placement.axis])
            index = [slice(None)] * grid.ndim
            index[placement.axis] = slice(start, start + size)
            grid[tuple(index)] = device
            start += size
    return grid


HOLDER = {ALL: "every device", PARTIAL: "every device, one term each"} | {
    d: f"device {d}" for d in range(4)
}
# One band per value from -2 to 3: partial, all, then devices 0 to 3.
BANDS = [
    viz.INK_SECONDARY,
    viz.GRID,
    viz.BLUE,
    viz.ORANGE,
    viz.AQUA,
    viz.YELLOW,
]
OWNER_SCALE = [
    [edge, color]
    for i, color in enumerate(BANDS)
    for edge in (i / 6, (i + 1) / 6)
]


def owner_heatmap(t: Tensor, show_values: bool = True, **trace) -> go.Heatmap:
    grid = owners(t)
    values = t.to_numpy()
    rows, cols = grid.shape
    holder = [
        [HOLDER[int(grid[r, c])] for c in range(cols)] for r in range(rows)
    ]
    return go.Heatmap(
        z=grid,
        zmin=-2.5,
        zmax=3.5,
        colorscale=OWNER_SCALE,
        showscale=False,
        xgap=2,
        ygap=2,
        customdata=holder,
        text=values.round(1) if show_values else None,
        texttemplate="%{text}" if show_values else None,
        hovertemplate="row %{y}, column %{x}<br>held by %{customdata}<extra></extra>",
        **trace,
    )


def device_legend(fig: go.Figure) -> None:
    for d in range(4):
        fig.add_trace(
            go.Scatter(
                x=[None],
                y=[None],
                mode="markers",
                name=f"device {d}",
                marker=dict(symbol="square", size=12, color=BANDS[d + 2]),
            )
        )

Move the slider to compare placements of the same 8 × 4 matrix:

placements = {
    "Sharded(0)": Sharded(0),
    "Sharded(1)": Sharded(1),
    "Replicated()": Replicated(),
}
steps = [[owner_heatmap(Tensor(a, device=on(p)))] for p in placements.values()]
fig = go.Figure(
    layout=dict(
        title="Which device holds each element of an 8 × 4 matrix",
        height=520,
        xaxis=dict(showgrid=False, dtick=1),
        yaxis=dict(showgrid=False, autorange="reversed", dtick=1),
    )
)
device_legend(fig)
viz.show(viz.with_slider(fig, steps, list(placements), "placement "))

to_numpy() gathers the shards back into one array, so checking a distributed result against NumPy is one line:

print(np.array_equal(x.to_numpy(), a))

True

Sharded matrix multiplication

Models spend most of their time in matrix multiplications, and there are two natural ways to split \(y = x W\) across devices. Every tensor-parallel transformer uses both.

In a column-parallel multiplication, every device holds all of \(x\) and a slice of \(W\)’s columns, and computes the matching slice of \(y\). It needs no communication:

w = np.random.default_rng(0).standard_normal((4, 6)).astype(np.float32)

y = Tensor(a, device=on(Replicated())) @ Tensor(w, device=on(Sharded(1)))
print(y.mapping)
print("correct:", np.allclose(y.to_numpy(), a @ w, atol=1e-5))
assert np.allclose(y.to_numpy(), a @ w, atol=1e-5)

DeviceMapping(DeviceMesh(tp=4), [Sharded(axis=1)])
correct: True

In a row-parallel multiplication, each device holds a slice of \(x\)’s columns and the matching slice of \(W\)’s rows. Each device’s product is one term of the full sum over the inner dimension, so the result is Partial:

z = Tensor(a, device=on(Sharded(1))) @ Tensor(w, device=on(Sharded(0)))
print(z.mapping)
print("correct:", np.allclose(z.to_numpy(), a @ w, atol=1e-5))
assert np.allclose(z.to_numpy(), a @ w, atol=1e-5)

DeviceMapping(DeviceMesh(tp=4), [Partial()])
correct: True

A Partial tensor is correct only once its terms are summed. Most operations, such as an activation, need the real value, so MAX must first combine the shards with a collective, an operation that communicates between devices. Inside auto_reshard(mode="warn"), MAX reports every collective it inserts and the reason for it:

with warnings.catch_warnings(record=True) as caught:
    warnings.simplefilter("always")
    with auto_reshard(mode="warn"):
        activated = F.relu(z)
print(next(w.message for w in caught if "reshards" in str(w.message)))
print("result:", activated.mapping)


relu(x) reshards x
  x      : float32[8, 6]@mesh(tp=4){"tp":P}
  region : auto_reshard(DEFAULT_TRANSITIONS, mode="warn")

  decided per mesh axis
  axis    x          -> out       collective         bytes   status
  "tp"    x{P->S0}   -> S0        reduce_scatter(x)     144  not allowed
          x{P->S1}   -> S1        reduce_scatter(x)     144  not allowed
          x{P->R}    -> R         allreduce(x)          288  chosen, reshards x
  result  x{"tp":R}  -> {"tp":R}

  x = transfer_to(x, DeviceMapping(mesh, (R,)))
  with auto_reshard(mode="silent"):
  with auto_reshard(ALL_TRANSITIONS, mode="silent"):   # also allows the cheaper rows above
  at /var/folders/gg/plf_qcqs5556rt10q1s96gl40000gn/T/ipykernel_87578/1282956743.py:4 in <module>()
result: DeviceMapping(DeviceMesh(tp=4), [Replicated()])

MAX chose an all-reduce: every device sends its partial sum and receives the total. It also considered a reduce-scatter, which sums and leaves each device one slice, but that transition isn’t in the default set. Outside an auto_reshard block, MAX raises an error instead of inserting a collective, so a model can’t add communication you didn’t plan for.

To move a tensor to a placement yourself, use F.transfer_to:

replicated = F.transfer_to(z, on(Replicated()))
print(replicated.mapping)

DeviceMapping(DeviceMesh(tp=4), [Replicated()])

Column then row: one all-reduce per block

Chaining the two patterns is the core of tensor parallelism, as introduced by the Megatron-LM paper. A column-parallel layer leaves its output split by columns, which is the input a row-parallel layer needs, so the pair runs with no communication between them and a single all-reduce at the end. Here is a small MLP block, checked against NumPy:

rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
w_up = rng.standard_normal((4, 16)).astype(np.float32)
w_down = rng.standard_normal((16, 4)).astype(np.float32)

with warnings.catch_warnings(record=True) as caught:
    warnings.simplefilter("always")
    with auto_reshard(mode="warn"):
        h = F.relu(
            Tensor(a, device=on(Replicated()))
            @ Tensor(w_up, device=on(Sharded(1)))
        )
        partial_out = h @ Tensor(w_down, device=on(Sharded(0)))
reshards = [w for w in caught if "reshards" in str(w.message)]
out = F.transfer_to(partial_out, on(Replicated()))  # the one all-reduce

print("hidden:", h.mapping, " block output:", partial_out.mapping)
print("collectives MAX had to insert:", len(reshards))
print(
    "correct:",
    np.allclose(out.to_numpy(), np.maximum(a @ w_up, 0) @ w_down, atol=1e-3),
)
assert not reshards and np.allclose(
    out.to_numpy(), np.maximum(a @ w_up, 0) @ w_down, atol=1e-3
)

hidden: DeviceMapping(DeviceMesh(tp=4), [Sharded(axis=1)])  block output: DeviceMapping(DeviceMesh(tp=4), [Partial()])
collectives MAX had to insert: 0
correct: True

Here is the whole block as placements. Every device holds all of x. The column-parallel weight and the hidden activation are split by columns in the same way, so each device multiplies only its own slice. The row-parallel weight is split by rows to match, and the block’s output, h W_down, is a partial sum on every device (dark gray) until the all-reduce sums it:

panels = {
    "x": Tensor(a, device=on(Replicated())),
    "W_up": Tensor(w_up, device=on(Sharded(1))),
    "h": h,
    "W_down": Tensor(w_down, device=on(Sharded(0))),
    "h W_down": partial_out,
    "summed": out,
}
widths = [int(t.shape[1]) for t in panels.values()]
fig = make_subplots(
    rows=1,
    cols=len(panels),
    column_widths=widths,
    horizontal_spacing=0.03,
    subplot_titles=list(panels),
)
for col, t in enumerate(panels.values(), start=1):
    fig.add_trace(owner_heatmap(t, show_values=False), row=1, col=col)
device_legend(fig)
fig.update_xaxes(showgrid=False, showticklabels=False)
fig.update_yaxes(showgrid=False, showticklabels=False, autorange="reversed")
fig.update_layout(
    title="A column-parallel, row-parallel MLP block on 4 devices",
    height=420,
    legend=dict(y=-0.04, yanchor="top"),
    margin=dict(t=96),
)
viz.show(fig)

The hidden activation stayed sharded, relu ran on each shard independently, and the only communication was the explicit all-reduce at the end. Section 2.2 applies this pattern to every attention and MLP block of SmolLM2.

Two-dimensional meshes

Real deployments combine strategies. A mesh with two axes can split a batch of requests across dp (data parallelism) while splitting each layer’s weights across tp. Each mesh axis gets its own placement:

mesh_2d = DeviceMesh((CPU(),) * 4, (2, 2), ("dp", "tp"))
batch = Tensor(
    np.ones((4, 6), np.float32),
    device=DeviceMapping(mesh_2d, (Sharded(0), Sharded(1))),
)
visualize(batch)

DType.float32 [4, 6] with DeviceMapping(DeviceMesh(dp=2, tp=2), [Sharded(axis=0), Sharded(axis=1)])
┌──────────┬──────────┐   tensor[:, :]
│ device 0 │ device 1 │   ├── dp=0 - tensor[:2, :]
│ [:2, :3] │ [:2, 3:] │   │   ├── tp=0  device 0 - tensor[:2, :3]
├──────────┼──────────┤   │   └── tp=1  device 1 - tensor[:2, 3:]
│ device 2 │ device 3 │   └── dp=1 - tensor[2:, :]
│ [2:, :3] │ [2:, 3:] │       ├── tp=0  device 2 - tensor[2:, :3]
└──────────┴──────────┘       └── tp=1  device 3 - tensor[2:, 3:]

On real hardware, the cost of each collective depends on how much data it moves and how fast the devices are connected, and that decides the best layout for a given model and cluster. The handbook’s parallelism strategies page compares data, tensor, pipeline, and expert parallelism. MAX’s docs cover distributed tensors in more detail.

Exercises

  1. Shard x with Sharded(1) instead of Sharded(0) and visualize it. What happens with 3 devices instead of 4, which don’t divide 4 columns evenly?
  2. Swap the order in the MLP block: row-parallel first, then column-parallel. Which collectives does MAX insert now?
  3. Count the bytes each device sends in the all-reduce of the MLP block. How does it scale with the batch size and the hidden size?

Next

Section 2.2 splits SmolLM2 across devices and checks that its logits don’t change.

2.2 A tensor-parallel model

This section splits your SmolLM2 from Chapter 1 across three devices with tensor parallelism, the strategy servers use to run a model too big or too slow for one GPU. The model code doesn’t change. You change where its weights live, and MAX runs every layer on all three devices and adds the communication. Then you check that the logits match the single-device model’s.

import logging
import time
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from max import functional as F
from max.driver import CPU
from max.dtype import DType
from max.nn.common_layers.linear import col_parallel, row_parallel
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer
from max.tensor import Tensor, default_device, default_dtype
from max.tensor.sharding import (
    DeviceMapping,
    DeviceMesh,
    Replicated,
    auto_reshard,
    visualize,
)
from max.tensor.tensor import TensorType

from minillm import viz
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import LlamaForCausalLM, load_model
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
weights = load_checkpoint()

The plan

Section 2.1 showed the pattern: a column-parallel layer followed by a row-parallel layer needs one all-reduce. Each SmolLM2 layer has two such pairs:

BlockColumn parallel (split outputs)Row parallel (split inputs)
Attentionq_proj, k_proj, v_projo_proj
MLPgate_proj, up_projdown_proj

Splitting q_proj’s outputs three ways gives each device 3 of the 9 query heads, and splitting k_proj and v_proj gives each device 1 of the 3 key/value heads, the one those queries share under grouped-query attention (Section 1.5). So attention runs on each device independently, on its own heads. The embedding, the norms, and the LM head stay whole on every device (replicated).

max.nn provides col_parallel and row_parallel, which change a Linear layer’s weight placement on the mesh axis you name:

from __future__ import annotations

from max.nn.common_layers.linear import col_parallel, row_parallel

from minillm.model import LlamaForCausalLM


def tensor_parallel(
    model: LlamaForCausalLM, axis: str = "tp"
) -> LlamaForCausalLM:
    """Shards every layer's projections across mesh axis ``axis``."""
    for layer in model.model.layers:
        attn, mlp = layer.self_attn, layer.mlp
        for linear in (
            attn.q_proj,
            attn.k_proj,
            attn.v_proj,
            mlp.gate_proj,
            mlp.up_proj,
        ):
            col_parallel(linear, axis)
        for linear in (attn.o_proj, mlp.down_proj):
            row_parallel(linear, axis)
    return model

Saved to minillm/parallel.py.

Build the model on a mesh

Build the model inside default_device(mesh) so its weights are created on the mesh, apply the plan, and load the checkpoint. load_state_dict cuts each weight into the shards its placement asks for:

mesh = DeviceMesh((CPU(),) * 3, (3,), ("tp",))

with F.lazy(), default_device(mesh), default_dtype(DType.float32):
    tp_model = tensor_parallel(LlamaForCausalLM(config))
tp_model.load_state_dict(
    {**weights, "lm_head.weight": weights["model.embed_tokens.weight"]}
)

visualize(tp_model.model.layers[0].self_attn.q_proj.weight)
visualize(tp_model.model.layers[0].self_attn.o_proj.weight)

Read the head assignment straight from the shards: each device’s slice of q_proj holds whole query heads, and its slice of k_proj holds the key/value head those queries share. Hover over a box to see its device:
