Chapter 5 uses what you measured here to build the core of a serving engine that batches many requests into each step.

5. A serving engine

The model from Chapter 1 generates for one prompt at a time. A serving engine takes requests from many users at once and keeps them all moving. This chapter builds the core of such an engine, one technique per section.

SectionYou learn
5.1 The life of a requestA batched model step for an engine, and a first engine that serves one request at a time.
5.2 Continuous batchingA scheduler, and three batching policies compared on the same workload.
5.3 A paged KV cacheBlocks and block tables, the idea behind MAX Serve’s paged KV cache, and the memory they save.
5.4 Prefix cachingSharing the cache blocks of a common prompt prefix between requests.
5.5 Speculative decodingDrafting tokens with SmolLM2-135M, verifying them with SmolLM2-360M, and when it pays off.

Related handbook pages: batching, PagedAttention, prefix caching, and speculative decoding.

5.1 The life of a request

So far your model served one prompt at a time, from a notebook. A serving engine takes requests from many users at once and keeps them all moving: it admits new requests, runs the model, hands each request its next token, and stops each one when it is done. This chapter builds that engine over five sections, on top of a new model interface designed for it.

MAX Serve and other inference engines share this structure:

requests ─► scheduler ─► model step (one batched forward pass) ─► sampler
               ▲                                                     │
               └─────────── KV cache manager ◄──── new tokens ◄──────┘

The handbook’s How does LLM inference work? page describes the same loop.

import logging
import math
import time
import warnings
from dataclasses import dataclass, field

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from max import functional as F
from max.driver import CPU
from max.dtype import DType
from max.nn import ModuleList
from max.nn.kernels.attention import flash_attention
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer
from max.tensor import Tensor
from max.tensor.tensor import TensorType
from plotly.subplots import make_subplots

from minillm import viz
from minillm.attention import Attention
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.layers import rotate_half
from minillm.model import DecoderLayer, LlamaForCausalLM, load_model
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)

A model step for an engine

The cached model from Section 1.8 kept its cache inside and handled one sequence. An engine needs one call that advances several sequences, each with its own history, by any number of tokens. The step model does that. Each call takes, for every sequence in the batch:

  • the new tokens and their positions,
  • an attention mask saying which past and new positions each token may see,
  • the past keys and values,

and returns the next-token logits plus only the new keys and values. The model keeps no state. Where the cache lives and how it is laid out is up to the engine, and Section 5.3 and Section 5.4 change exactly that.

Positions now differ per sequence, so RoPE takes a position for each token instead of a start offset:

from __future__ import annotations

import math
from collections.abc import Callable

from max import functional as F
from max.dtype import DType
from max.nn import ModuleList
from max.nn.kernels.attention import flash_attention
from max.tensor import Tensor

from minillm.attention import Attention
from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.layers import rotate_half
from minillm.model import DecoderLayer, LlamaForCausalLM

KVPair = tuple[Tensor, Tensor]
Step = Callable[..., tuple[Tensor, list[KVPair]]]
# Masked scores use a large finite value instead of -inf: attention kernels
# process keys in blocks, and a row whose first block is fully -inf computes
# exp(-inf - -inf) = NaN, which then spreads through the values to other rows.
MASKED = -1e30


def rope_at(
    x: Tensor, positions: Tensor, head_dim: int, theta: float
) -> Tensor:
    """Rotates ``x``, ``[batch, q, heads, head_dim]``, for per-token
    ``positions``, ``[batch, q]``."""
    inv_freq = Tensor(
        [theta ** (-i / head_dim) for i in range(0, head_dim, 2)],
        dtype=DType.float32,
        device=x.mesh,
    )
    batch, q = positions.shape
    angles = positions.reshape([batch, q, 1]) * inv_freq
    angles = F.concat([angles, angles], axis=-1).reshape(
        [batch, q, 1, head_dim]
    )
    return x * F.cos(angles) + rotate_half(x) * F.sin(angles)


class StepAttention(Attention):
    """Attends from new tokens to the past keys and values plus their own."""

    def forward(
        self, x: Tensor, positions: Tensor, mask: Tensor, past: KVPair
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, KVPair]:
        batch, q, _ = x.shape
        query = self.q_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, q, self.num_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        key = self.k_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, q, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        value = self.v_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, q, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        query = rope_at(query, positions, self.head_dim, self.rope_theta)
        key = rope_at(key, positions, self.head_dim, self.rope_theta)
        all_keys = F.concat([past[0], key], axis=1)
        all_values = F.concat([past[1], value], axis=1)
        # The mask has one column per past and new position.
        mask = F.rebind(mask, [batch, q, all_keys.shape[1]])
        out = flash_attention(
            query, all_keys, all_values, 1 / math.sqrt(self.head_dim), mask
        ).rebind_mapping(query.mapping)
        out = out.reshape([batch, q, self.num_heads * self.head_dim])
        return self.o_proj(out), (key, value)


class StepLayer(DecoderLayer):
    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.self_attn = StepAttention(config)

    def forward(
        self, x: Tensor, positions: Tensor, mask: Tensor, past: KVPair
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, KVPair]:
        attn, kv = self.self_attn(
            self.input_layernorm(x), positions, mask, past
        )
        x = x + attn
        return x + self.mlp(self.post_attention_layernorm(x)), kv


class StepModel(LlamaForCausalLM):
    """Advances a batch of sequences: returns each one's next-token logits
    and the new tokens' keys and values for every layer."""

    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.model.layers = ModuleList(
            [StepLayer(config) for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)]
        )

    def forward(
        self,
        tokens: Tensor,
        positions: Tensor,
        mask: Tensor,
        past: list[KVPair],
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, list[KVPair]]:
        x = self.model.embed_tokens(tokens)
        new_kv = []
        for layer, layer_past in zip(self.model.layers, past, strict=True):
            x, kv = layer(x, positions, mask, layer_past)
            new_kv.append(kv)
        return self.lm_head(self.model.norm(x)[:, -1]), new_kv

Saved to minillm/step.py.

Compile it once. Every dimension that changes between calls is symbolic: the batch size, the number of new tokens q, and the past length:

import numpy as np
from max.driver import Device
from max.tensor.tensor import TensorType


def compile_step(model: StepModel, config: LlamaConfig, device: Device) -> Step:
    """Compiles ``model`` for any batch size, number of new tokens, and past
    length."""
    kv = TensorType(
        DType.float32,
        ("batch", "past", config.num_key_value_heads, config.head_dim),
        device=device,
    )
    return model.compile(
        TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "q"), device=device),
        TensorType(DType.float32, ("batch", "q"), device=device),
        TensorType(DType.float32, ("batch", "q", "kv"), device=device),
        [(kv, kv)] * config.num_hidden_layers,
    )

Saved to minillm/step.py.

start = time.perf_counter()
weights = load_checkpoint()
step = compile_step(
    load_model(config, weights, DEVICE, StepModel), config, DEVICE
)
print(f"compiled the step model in {time.perf_counter() - start:.0f} s")

The cache and the batch

The engine stores each sequence’s keys and values in a cache object. The first version is the simplest: one growing array per layer. Every later cache has the same interface (length, num_layers, read, and append), so run_step and the step model work with all of them unchanged.

class ContiguousCache:
    """One sequence's keys and values: a growing array per layer."""

    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        shape = (0, config.num_key_value_heads, config.head_dim)
        self.layers = [
            [np.zeros(shape, np.float32), np.zeros(shape, np.float32)]
            for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)
        ]

    @property
    def length(self) -> int:
        return len(self.layers[0][0])

    @property
    def num_layers(self) -> int:
        return len(self.layers)

    def read(self, layer: int) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:
        return self.layers[layer][0], self.layers[layer][1]

    def append(self, layer: int, keys: np.ndarray, values: np.ndarray) -> None:
        entry = self.layers[layer]
        entry[0] = np.concatenate([entry[0], keys])
        entry[1] = np.concatenate([entry[1], values])

Saved to minillm/step.py.

To run several sequences in one step, the engine packs them into rectangular arrays. Sequences add different numbers of new tokens, so shorter ones are left-padded, and the mask is built per token: a new token may see its own sequence’s past and the new tokens up to itself, and nothing else. A padding token sees only itself, so its row of the softmax stays well defined; its output is discarded. Masked positions get a large negative number instead of \(-\infty\). If a padding row’s first block of keys were entirely \(-\infty\), a blockwise attention kernel would compute \(e^{-\infty + \infty}\), which is NaN. In the next layer that NaN would become the padding position’s keys and values, and the real tokens of the same sequence would pick it up even though the mask hides that position.

def run_step(
    step: Step, caches: list, new_tokens: list[list[int]], device: Device
) -> np.ndarray:
    """Advances each sequence by its ``new_tokens``, appends their keys and
    values to its cache, and returns next-token logits, ``[batch, vocab]``."""
    batch, q = len(caches), max(len(t) for t in new_tokens)
    past = max(c.length for c in caches)
    tokens = np.zeros((batch, q), np.int64)
    positions = np.zeros((batch, q), np.float32)
    mask = np.full((batch, q, past + q), MASKED, np.float32)
    for i, (cache, new) in enumerate(zip(caches, new_tokens, strict=True)):
        pad, n = q - len(new), cache.length
        tokens[i, pad:] = new
        positions[i, pad:] = np.arange(n, n + len(new))
        mask[i, pad:, :n] = 0.0  # every new token sees the sequence's past
        mask[i, pad:, past + pad :] = np.triu(
            np.full((len(new), len(new)), MASKED), 1
        )
        mask[i, np.arange(pad), past + np.arange(pad)] = (
            0.0  # padding sees itself
        )
    past_kv = []
    for layer in range(caches[0].num_layers):
        keys = np.zeros(
            (batch, past, *caches[0].read(layer)[0].shape[1:]), np.float32
        )
        values = np.zeros_like(keys)
        for i, cache in enumerate(caches):
            k, v = cache.read(layer)
            keys[i, : len(k)], values[i, : len(v)] = k, v
        past_kv.append(
            (Tensor(keys, device=device), Tensor(values, device=device))
        )
    logits, new_kv = step(
        Tensor(tokens, device=device),
        Tensor(positions, device=device),
        Tensor(mask, device=device),
        past_kv,
    )
    for layer, (k, v) in enumerate(new_kv):
        k, v = k.to_numpy(), v.to_numpy()
        for i, (cache, new) in enumerate(zip(caches, new_tokens, strict=True)):
            pad = q - len(new)
            cache.append(layer, k[i, pad:], v[i, pad:])
    return logits.to_numpy()

Saved to minillm/step.py.

Check the step against the model from Section 1.6 by generating greedily for two prompts of different lengths in the same steps:

full = load_model(config, weights, DEVICE).compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq"), device=DEVICE)
)


def greedy_reference(ids: list[int], n: int) -> list[int]:
    ids = list(ids)
    for _ in range(n):
        ids.append(
            int(
                full(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE))
                .to_numpy()[0, -1]
                .argmax()
            )
        )
    return ids[-n:]


prompts = [
    tokenizer([t])["input_ids"][0]
    for t in ["The capital of France is", "Once upon a time, there was a"]
]
caches = [ContiguousCache(config) for _ in prompts]
outputs = [[int(t)] for t in run_step(step, caches, prompts, DEVICE).argmax(-1)]
for _ in range(11):
    next_ids = run_step(
        step, caches, [[o[-1]] for o in outputs], DEVICE
    ).argmax(-1)
    for out, t in zip(outputs, next_ids):
        out.append(int(t))
for prompt, out in zip(prompts, outputs):
    print(out == greedy_reference(prompt, 12), repr(tokenizer.decode(out)))
    assert out == greedy_reference(prompt, 12)

True ' Paris. Paris is the largest city in France and the capital'
True ' young woman named Sarah who was struggling to find her place in'

Both sequences match the reference, even though they have different lengths and shared every step.

Here is the mask run_step builds for a mixed step: sequence 0 has 6 tokens cached and decodes 1 new token, while sequence 1 has nothing cached and prefills a 4-token prompt. A wrapper around step records the inputs it receives. Blue cells may attend; hover over any cell:

recorded = {}

    recorded.update(
        tokens=tokens.to_numpy(),
        positions=positions.to_numpy(),
        mask=mask.to_numpy(),

    return step(tokens, positions, mask, past)

Sequence 0’s single new token sits in the last row, after three padding rows that each see only themselves. It sees its 6 cached slots and itself. Sequence 1 has no cached tokens, so its 6 cached slots are masked in every row. They hold zeros, there only because the batch’s longest past is 6. Its 4 new tokens form the triangle of the causal mask.

A first engine: one request at a time

A request carries its prompt, how many tokens it may generate, and timestamps for the latency metrics from Section 1.1, such as time to first token (TTFT). The simplest engine serves requests in arrival order, each to completion, one at a time:

from dataclasses import dataclass, field


@dataclass
class Request:
    prompt: list[int]
    max_new_tokens: int
    output: list[int] = field(default_factory=list)
    arrived: float = 0.0
    first_token: float | None = None
    finished: float | None = None

    @property
    def done(self) -> bool:
        return len(self.output) >= self.max_new_tokens

Saved to minillm/step.py.

def serve_one_at_a_time(requests: list[Request]) -> None:
    for request in requests:
        cache = ContiguousCache(config)
        logits = run_step(step, [cache], [request.prompt], DEVICE)
        while True:
            request.output.append(int(logits[0].argmax()))
            request.first_token = request.first_token or time.perf_counter()
            if request.done:
                break
            logits = run_step(step, [cache], [[request.output[-1]]], DEVICE)
        request.finished = time.perf_counter()


def make_workload() -> list[Request]:
    texts = [
        "Tell me about Paris.",
        "Write a haiku about the sea.",
        "What is 2 + 2?",
        "List three colors.",
        "Explain gravity in one sentence.",
        "Name a fruit.",
        "Describe a cat.",
        "Say hello in French.",
    ]
    now = time.perf_counter()
    return [
        Request(tokenizer([t])["input_ids"][0], max_new_tokens=n, arrived=now)
        for t, n in zip(texts, [32, 24, 8, 16, 32, 8, 24, 16])
    ]


def report(requests: list[Request]) -> None:
    ttft = [(r.first_token - r.arrived) * 1000 for r in requests]
    total = max(r.finished for r in requests) - min(r.arrived for r in requests)
    tokens = sum(len(r.output) for r in requests)
    print(
        f"TTFT: first request {ttft[0]:.0f} ms, median {np.median(ttft):.0f} ms, worst {max(ttft):.0f} ms"
    )
    print(f"{tokens} tokens in {total:.1f} s = {tokens / total:.0f} tokens/s")


sequential = make_workload()
serve_one_at_a_time(sequential)
report(sequential)

TTFT: first request 317 ms, median 25305 ms, worst 50618 ms
160 tokens in 56.2 s = 3 tokens/s

Eight users sent their requests at the same moment. The first one got a token almost at once. The last one waited for every request ahead of it to finish, so its time to first token is most of the total run time. Section 4.3 measured a decoder layer’s decode step for 32 sequences at about 1.2 to 1.3 times the cost of a step for one, on the Mac the book was built on, so this engine leaves most of the hardware’s capacity unused. Section 5.2 runs several requests in each step.

The handbook’s LLM inference metrics page explains why serving systems track TTFT percentiles as well as averages.

Exercises

  1. Serve the workload in order of max_new_tokens, shortest first. What happens to the median and worst TTFT? What would a user with a long request think of this policy?
  2. Run run_step with three prompts in one call and check each against greedy_reference.
  3. Print the mask run_step builds for two sequences with 2 and 4 new tokens and past lengths 3 and 1. Check each row by hand.

Next

Section 5.2 replaces the one-at-a-time loop with continuous batching.

5.2 Continuous batching

The engine from Section 5.1 ran one sequence per step, while Section 4.3 showed that a decode step for many sequences costs little more than a step for one. Batching uses that spare capacity, and the way you batch decides who waits. This section adds a scheduler to the engine and compares three policies on the same workload.

import logging
import time
import warnings
from collections import deque

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from max.driver import CPU
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.step import (
    ContiguousCache,
    Request,
    StepModel,
    compile_step,
    run_step,
)
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)
step = compile_step(
    load_model(config, load_checkpoint(), DEVICE, StepModel), config, DEVICE
)

The engine

The engine holds two lists: requests waiting to start and requests running. Each call to step does one model step:

  • If there is room in the batch and requests are waiting, it admits them and runs their prefill: all their prompt tokens, in one batched step.
  • Otherwise it runs a decode step: one new token for every running request, in one batched step.

Finished requests leave the batch after every step. That is continuous batching: the batch is re-formed at every step, so a waiting request starts as soon as a slot frees up. Setting continuous=False gives static batching instead: a batch is admitted together and must finish completely before the next one starts.

Running prefill and decode in separate steps is a simple policy that gives new requests priority, at the cost of pausing the running requests’ decode for a step. Production engines can also split a long prompt into chunks (chunked prefill) and run prefill chunks and decodes in the same step, so one big prefill doesn’t stall everyone. MAX Serve chunks long prefills by default (--enable-chunked-prefill) and batches them with decodes when you pass --enable-in-flight-batching.

from __future__ import annotations

import time
from collections import deque
from collections.abc import Callable

from max.driver import Device

from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.step import ContiguousCache, Request, Step, run_step


class Engine:
    """Serves requests with a batching scheduler and greedy decoding."""

    def __init__(
        self,
        step: Step,
        config: LlamaConfig,
        device: Device,
        max_batch: int = 4,
        continuous: bool = True,
        new_cache: Callable[[LlamaConfig], object] = ContiguousCache,
    ) -> None:
        self.step_fn, self.config, self.device = step, config, device
        self.max_batch, self.continuous, self.new_cache = (
            max_batch,
            continuous,
            new_cache,
        )
        self.waiting: deque[Request] = deque()
        self.running: list[tuple[Request, object]] = []
        self.steps = 0

    def add(self, request: Request) -> None:
        self.waiting.append(request)

    def has_work(self) -> bool:
        return bool(self.waiting or self.running)

    def step(self) -> None:
        room = self.max_batch - len(self.running)
        can_admit = (
            self.waiting and room > 0 and (self.continuous or not self.running)
        )
        if can_admit:
            admitted = [
                self.waiting.popleft()
                for _ in range(min(room, len(self.waiting)))
            ]
            batch = [(r, self.new_cache(self.config)) for r in admitted]
            inputs = [self.prefill_tokens(r, c) for r, c in batch]
        else:
            batch = self.running
            inputs = [[r.output[-1]] for r, _ in batch]
        logits = run_step(
            self.step_fn, [c for _, c in batch], inputs, self.device
        )
        self.steps += 1
        now = time.perf_counter()
        for (request, _), token in zip(batch, logits.argmax(-1), strict=True):
            request.output.append(int(token))
            if request.first_token is None:
                request.first_token = now
            if request.done:
                request.finished = now
        if can_admit:
            for request, cache in batch:
                self.prefilled(request, cache)
            self.running += batch
        self.running = [(r, c) for r, c in self.running if not r.done]
        self.release_finished(batch)

    def prefill_tokens(self, request: Request, cache: object) -> list[int]:
        """Returns the prompt tokens to compute; Section 5.4 skips cached ones."""
        return request.prompt

    def prefilled(self, request: Request, cache: object) -> None:
        """Runs after a request's prefill; Section 5.4 uses it."""

    def release_finished(self, batch: list) -> None:
        """Frees the caches of finished requests; Section 5.3 needs this."""

    def run(self) -> None:
        while self.has_work():
            self.step()

Saved to minillm/engine.py.

A workload

Eight requests arrive at once, each asking for between 8 and 32 new tokens. The workload is the same as in Section 5.1:

TEXTS = [
    "Tell me about Paris.",
    "Write a haiku about the sea.",
    "What is 2 + 2?",
    "List three colors.",
    "Explain gravity in one sentence.",
    "Name a fruit.",
    "Describe a cat.",
    "Say hello in French.",
]
LENGTHS = [32, 24, 8, 16, 32, 8, 24, 16]


def run_policy(
    max_batch: int, continuous: bool
) -> tuple[list[Request], Engine]:
    engine = Engine(
        step, config, DEVICE, max_batch=max_batch, continuous=continuous
    )
    now = time.perf_counter()
    requests = [
        Request(tokenizer([t])["input_ids"][0], n, arrived=now)
        for t, n in zip(TEXTS, LENGTHS)
    ]
    for r in requests:
        engine.add(r)
    engine.run()
    return requests, engine


def summarize(name: str, requests: list[Request], engine: Engine) -> dict:
    ttft = [(r.first_token - r.arrived) * 1000 for r in requests]
    total = max(r.finished for r in requests) - min(r.arrived for r in requests)
    tokens = sum(len(r.output) for r in requests)
    print(
        f"{name:22} {engine.steps:4} steps  {tokens / total:5.1f} tokens/s  "
        f"TTFT median {np.median(ttft):6.0f} ms  worst {max(ttft):6.0f} ms"
    )
    return {
        "ttft": ttft,
        "outputs": [r.output for r in requests],
        "requests": requests,
    }


results = {}
for name, max_batch, continuous in [
    ("one at a time", 1, True),
    ("static batches of 4", 4, False),
    ("continuous, batch 4", 4, True),
]:
    results[name] = summarize(name, *run_policy(max_batch, continuous))

one at a time           160 steps    3.6 tokens/s  TTFT median  20020 ms  worst  39529 ms
static batches of 4      64 steps    8.5 tokens/s  TTFT median   4942 ms  worst   9643 ms
continuous, batch 4      50 steps   11.3 tokens/s  TTFT median   1334 ms  worst   7702 ms

Batching changes only the order of the work, so greedy decoding must produce the same tokens for every request under every policy:

reference = results["one at a time"]["outputs"]
print(all(r["outputs"] == reference for r in results.values()))
assert all(r["outputs"] == reference for r in results.values())

True

steps = []
for name, r in results.items():
    start = min(q.arrived for q in r["requests"])
    rows = [f"request {i} ({n} tokens)" for i, n in enumerate(LENGTHS)]
    wait = [(q.first_token - q.arrived) * 1000 for q in r["requests"]]
    gen = [(q.finished - q.first_token) * 1000 for q in r["requests"]]
    first = [(q.first_token - start) * 1000 for q in r["requests"]]
    steps.append(
        [
            go.Bar(
                y=rows,
                x=wait,
                base=[(q.arrived - start) * 1000 for q in r["requests"]],
                orientation="h",
                name="waiting, then prefill",
                marker_color=viz.MUTED,
                legendgroup="wait",
                hovertemplate="%{y}: first token after %{x:.0f} ms<extra></extra>",
            ),
            go.Bar(
                y=rows,
                x=gen,
                base=first,
                orientation="h",
                name="generating",
                marker_color=viz.BLUE,
                legendgroup="gen",
                hovertemplate="%{y}: generating for %{x:.0f} ms<extra></extra>",
            ),
        ]
    )
fig = go.Figure(
    layout=dict(
        title="Each request's life under each policy",
        height=460,
        barmode="overlay",
        xaxis_title="milliseconds since the requests arrived",
        yaxis=dict(autorange="reversed", showgrid=False),
    )
)
viz.show(viz.with_slider(fig, steps, list(results), "policy: ", active=2))

Slide between the policies. One at a time, each request waits for every request before it. Static batches run in two rounds, and the second round can’t start until the slowest request of the first finishes. Continuous batching starts a waiting request as soon as any slot frees up.

The same data as a distribution of time to first token:

fig = go.Figure(
    [
        go.Scatter(
            x=sorted(r["ttft"]),
            y=np.arange(1, 9) / 8,
            name=name,
            mode="lines+markers",
            line=dict(color=color, shape="hv"),
            hovertemplate=name
            + ": %{y:.0%} of requests within %{x:.0f} ms<extra></extra>",
        )
        for (name, r), color in zip(results.items(), viz.SERIES)
    ]
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Time to first token across the 8 requests",
    height=400,
    xaxis_title="time to first token (ms)",
    yaxis=dict(title="fraction of requests", tickformat=".0%", range=[0, 1.05]),
)
viz.show(fig)

Read the second chart as “what fraction of users had their first token within this time”. Both batching policies cut the number of model steps, from 160 to 64 for static batches and 50 for continuous batching, so they finish the workload sooner and raise throughput. They differ in who waits:

  • Static batching admits four requests and runs until the longest of them finishes. The second group waits for that, even after the short requests in the first group are done and their slots sit empty.
  • Continuous batching refills a slot as soon as a request finishes, so waiting requests start sooner, and the batch stays full while any request is waiting.

Production engines, MAX Serve among them, use continuous batching with much larger batches, bounded by the memory the KV cache needs. That bound is the subject of Section 5.3. The handbook’s static, dynamic, and continuous batching page compares the policies in production terms.

Exercises

  1. Run continuous batching with max_batch 2, 4, and 8. How do throughput and worst-case TTFT change?
  2. Make requests arrive over time: add each one after a few engine steps instead of all at the start. Which policy handles bursts better?
  3. Implement a mixed step: admit new requests’ prefills in the same step as running requests’ decodes. What does it cost the running requests?

Next

Section 5.3 replaces the contiguous cache with a paged one, so the batch can grow without wasting memory.

5.3 A paged KV cache

With continuous batching, memory limits how many requests can be in flight: every request needs room for its keys and values, and Section 1.8 measured 45 KiB per token for SmolLM2-135M in float32. The question is how to hand out that memory. A server doesn’t know how long a reply will be when it starts, so the simplest scheme reserves room for the maximum length, and most of that room goes unused. (ContiguousCache avoids the reservation by copying its arrays on every append, which costs time instead.) This section replaces the contiguous cache with a paged one, the idea behind vLLM’s PagedAttention and MAX Serve’s paged KV cache.

import logging
import time
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from max.driver import CPU
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.engine import Engine
from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.step import Request, StepModel, compile_step
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)
step = compile_step(
    load_model(config, load_checkpoint(), DEVICE, StepModel), config, DEVICE
)

Blocks and block tables

Paging works like virtual memory. The cache memory is one pool of fixed-size blocks, each holding the keys and values of block_size tokens. A sequence owns a list of block numbers, its block table, and takes a new block from the pool only when its last block fills. A sequence’s blocks needn’t be next to each other, so free memory never breaks into gaps too small to use; the only waste is the unfilled part of each sequence’s last block. A finished sequence returns its blocks to the pool at once.

from __future__ import annotations

import numpy as np

from minillm.config import LlamaConfig


class BlockPool:
    """Fixed-size blocks of KV cache memory, shared by all sequences."""

    def __init__(
        self, config: LlamaConfig, num_blocks: int, block_size: int = 16
    ) -> None:
        self.block_size = block_size
        shape = (
            num_blocks,
            block_size,
            config.num_key_value_heads,
            config.head_dim,
        )
        self.keys = [
            np.zeros(shape, np.float32) for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)
        ]
        self.values = [
            np.zeros(shape, np.float32) for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)
        ]
        self.free = list(range(num_blocks))

    @property
    def num_blocks(self) -> int:
        return len(self.keys[0])

    def allocate(self) -> int:
        if not self.free:
            raise MemoryError("the KV cache pool is full")
        return self.free.pop()

    def available(self) -> int:
        """Returns how many blocks ``allocate`` can still hand out."""
        return len(self.free)

    def release(self, blocks: list[int]) -> None:
        self.free.extend(blocks)


class PagedCache:
    """One sequence's keys and values, stored in pool blocks."""

    def __init__(self, pool: BlockPool) -> None:
        self.pool = pool
        self.blocks: list[int] = []
        self.length = 0

    @property
    def num_layers(self) -> int:
        return len(self.pool.keys)

    def read(self, layer: int) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:
        keys = self.pool.keys[layer][self.blocks].reshape(
            -1, *self.pool.keys[layer].shape[2:]
        )
        values = self.pool.values[layer][self.blocks].reshape(keys.shape)
        return keys[: self.length], values[: self.length]

    def append(self, layer: int, keys: np.ndarray, values: np.ndarray) -> None:
        size, start = self.pool.block_size, self.length
        if layer == 0:  # every layer stores the same positions
            while len(self.blocks) * size < start + len(keys):
                self.blocks.append(self.pool.allocate())
        for i in range(len(keys)):
            block, slot = self.blocks[(start + i) // size], (start + i) % size
            self.pool.keys[layer][block, slot] = keys[i]
            self.pool.values[layer][block, slot] = values[i]
        if layer == self.num_layers - 1:
            self.length += len(keys)

    def release(self) -> None:
        self.pool.release(self.blocks)
        self.blocks = []

Saved to minillm/paged.py.

read gathers a sequence’s blocks into one array for the step model. That copy keeps the code simple. Production engines skip it: their attention kernel reads the blocks in place through the block table, which takes a custom kernel like the ones Section 3.1 shows how to write. The engine needs three changes. It admits waiting requests in arrival order while the pool has blocks for their prompts. When a decode step needs new blocks and the pool is empty, it preempts the newest running request: it frees that request’s blocks and puts it back at the front of the queue, to recompute its prompt and the tokens it has generated so far when it runs again. And it returns a finished request’s blocks to the pool.

from minillm.engine import Engine
from minillm.step import Request


class PagedEngine(Engine):
    """An engine whose requests share one pool of KV cache blocks."""

    def __init__(self, *args, pool: BlockPool, **kwargs) -> None:
        super().__init__(*args, new_cache=lambda _: PagedCache(pool), **kwargs)
        self.pool = pool
        self.preemptions = 0

    def step(self) -> None:
        # Admit waiting requests in arrival order while their tokens so far fit.
        spare, admitted = self.pool.available(), 0
        for request in self.waiting:
            if self.blocks_needed(request) > spare:
                break
            spare -= self.blocks_needed(request)
            admitted += 1
        held = [self.waiting.pop() for _ in range(len(self.waiting) - admitted)]
        prefill = (
            self.waiting
            and len(self.running) < self.max_batch
            and (self.continuous or not self.running)
        )
        if not prefill and not self.running and held:
            raise MemoryError(
                "a waiting request needs more blocks than the pool has"
            )
        if not prefill:
            held = self.preempt_until_decode_fits() + held
        super().step()
        self.waiting.extend(held)

    def preempt_until_decode_fits(self) -> list[Request]:
        """Frees the newest requests' blocks until every running request can
        store its next token, and returns them to recompute later."""
        preempted = []
        while self.blocks_to_grow() > self.pool.available():
            if len(self.running) == 1:
                raise MemoryError(
                    "a request needs more blocks than the pool has"
                )
            request, cache = self.running.pop()
            cache.release()
            preempted.insert(0, request)
            self.preemptions += 1
        return preempted

    def blocks_to_grow(self) -> int:
        size = self.pool.block_size
        return sum(c.length + 1 > len(c.blocks) * size for _, c in self.running)

    def blocks_needed(self, request: Request) -> int:
        # A preempted request recomputes the tokens it already generated.
        tokens = len(request.prompt) + len(request.output)
        return -(-tokens // self.pool.block_size)

    def prefill_tokens(self, request: Request, cache: PagedCache) -> list[int]:
        return request.prompt + request.output

    def release_finished(self, batch: list) -> None:
        for request, cache in batch:
            if request.done:
                cache.release()

Saved to minillm/paged.py.

Check it

Paging only changes where the keys and values are stored. Serve the workload from Section 5.2 with a paged cache and compare the tokens:

TEXTS = [
    "Tell me about Paris.",
    "Write a haiku about the sea.",
    "What is 2 + 2?",
    "List three colors.",
    "Explain gravity in one sentence.",
    "Name a fruit.",
    "Describe a cat.",
    "Say hello in French.",
]
LENGTHS = [32, 24, 8, 16, 32, 8, 24, 16]


def workload() -> list[Request]:
    now = time.perf_counter()
    return [
        Request(tokenizer([t])["input_ids"][0], n, arrived=now)
        for t, n in zip(TEXTS, LENGTHS)
    ]


contiguous_requests = workload()
engine = Engine(step, config, DEVICE, max_batch=4)
for r in contiguous_requests:
    engine.add(r)
engine.run()

pool = BlockPool(config, num_blocks=32, block_size=16)
paged_requests = workload()
paged = PagedEngine(step, config, DEVICE, max_batch=4, pool=pool)
for r in paged_requests:
    paged.add(r)
paged.run()

print(
    "same tokens:",
    [r.output for r in paged_requests]
    == [r.output for r in contiguous_requests],
)
print("blocks free after the run:", len(pool.free), "of", pool.num_blocks)
assert [r.output for r in paged_requests] == [
    r.output for r in contiguous_requests
]
assert len(pool.free) == pool.num_blocks

same tokens: True
blocks free after the run: 32 of 32

The tokens match, and every block is back in the pool after the run. To watch the blocks move, run the workload again on a smaller pool of 24 blocks of 8 tokens, and record after every engine step which request owns each block. Slide through the steps:

class RecordingEngine(PagedEngine):
    def step(self) -> None:
        super().step()
        owner = np.full(self.pool.num_blocks, -1)
        for request, cache in self.running:
            owner[cache.blocks] = requests.index(request)
        self.snapshots.append(owner)


requests = workload()
recorder = RecordingEngine(
    step, config, DEVICE, max_batch=4, pool=BlockPool(config, 24, 8)
)
recorder.snapshots = []
for r in requests:
    recorder.add(r)
recorder.run()
print("preemptions:", recorder.preemptions)

colors = [viz.GRID] + viz.SERIES  # free, then one color per request
scale = [
    [edge, c] for i, c in enumerate(colors) for edge in (i / 9, (i + 1) / 9)
]
frames = []
for owner in recorder.snapshots:
    grid = owner.reshape(3, 8)
    text = [["" if o < 0 else f"r{o}" for o in row] for row in grid]
    hover = [
        [
            f"block {r * 8 + c}: " + ("free" if o < 0 else f"request {o}")
            for c, o in enumerate(row)
        ]
        for r, row in enumerate(grid)
    ]
    frames.append(
        [
            go.Heatmap(
                z=grid,
                zmin=-1.5,
                zmax=7.5,
                colorscale=scale,
                showscale=False,
                xgap=3,
                ygap=3,
                text=text,
                texttemplate="%{text}",
                customdata=hover,
                hovertemplate="%{customdata}<extra></extra>",
            )

    )
fig = go.Figure(
    layout=dict(
        title="Who owns each of the pool's 24 blocks",
        height=380,
        xaxis=dict(visible=False),
        yaxis=dict(visible=False, autorange="reversed"),
    )
)

viz.show(
    viz.with_slider(
        fig, frames, labels, "after engine step ", active=len(frames) // 3
    )
)

preemptions: 0

Blocks are handed out from wherever they are free, so a request’s blocks end up scattered across the pool, and a finished request’s blocks go straight to the next request. Only the block table records their order. This pool was big enough that no request was preempted; exercise 1 shrinks it until some are.

Why it matters: memory

Compare the memory each scheme ties up for a batch of four of these requests. A contiguous cache can’t grow in place, so when a request starts it must reserve room for the longest the request could get. That is SmolLM2’s full 8192-token context, or at best the request’s max_tokens limit, which API clients often set well above the length of the replies they get; the table below assumes 512. Paging allocates only what each sequence holds, rounded up to whole blocks of \(B\) tokens:

\[\text{blocks for } n \text{ tokens} = \left\lceil \frac{n}{B} \right\rceil\]

per_token = (
    2
    * config.num_hidden_layers
    * config.num_key_value_heads
    * config.head_dim
    * 4
)
batch = 4
actual = [len(r.prompt) + len(r.output) for r in paged_requests]
reserve_max_context = batch * config.max_position_embeddings
reserve_max_tokens = batch * (max(len(r.prompt) for r in paged_requests) + 512)
paged_tokens = sum(sorted(-(-n // 16) * 16 for n in actual)[-batch:])

print(f"{'scheme':38} {'tokens reserved':>15} {'memory':>9}")
for name, tokens in [
    ("reserve full context per request", reserve_max_context),
    ("reserve max_tokens=512 per request", reserve_max_tokens),
    ("paged, 16-token blocks (4 largest)", paged_tokens),
]:
    print(f"{name:38} {tokens:>15,} {tokens * per_token / 2**20:>7.1f} MiB")

schemes = {
    "reserve full context": reserve_max_context,
    "reserve max_tokens=512": reserve_max_tokens,
    "paged, 16-token blocks": paged_tokens,
}
fig = go.Figure(
    go.Bar(
        y=list(schemes),
        x=[t * per_token / 2**20 for t in schemes.values()],
        orientation="h",
        marker_color=[viz.ORANGE, viz.YELLOW, viz.BLUE],
        text=[f"{t * per_token / 2**20:,.1f} MiB" for t in schemes.values()],
        textposition="outside",
        cliponaxis=False,
        hovertemplate="%{y}: %{x:,.1f} MiB<extra></extra>",
    )
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="KV cache memory for a batch of 4 of these requests",
    height=300,
    margin=dict(r=96),
    xaxis=dict(title="MiB (log scale)", type="log", dtick=1),
    yaxis=dict(autorange="reversed", showgrid=False),
)
viz.show(fig)

scheme                                 tokens reserved    memory
reserve full context per request                32,768  1440.0 MiB
reserve max_tokens=512 per request               2,080    91.4 MiB
paged, 16-token blocks (4 largest)                 160     7.0 MiB

Saved memory becomes batch capacity. In the memory that one full-context reservation needs (8192 tokens), a paged cache fits about two hundred of these requests at roughly 40 tokens each, which is why most serving engines page their KV cache. The block size is a trade-off: bigger blocks waste more space in each sequence’s last block, and smaller ones mean longer block tables and more bookkeeping per step.

MAX Serve’s paged cache works the same way, and its attention kernels read the pages through the block table directly. Its scheduler also preempts a running request when the pool runs out of pages, and logs a warning that suggests giving the cache more memory. max serve exposes the settings. --kv-cache-page-size sets the block size, 128 tokens by default. --device-memory-utilization sets the fraction of device memory the process may use, 0.95 by default, and the KV cache pool gets what is left after the weights and other allocations. The pool size and --max-batch-size together limit how many requests run at once. The handbook’s PagedAttention page describes the algorithm, and GPU memory for LLMs shows how to size the pool.

Exercises

  1. Shrink the pool to 8 blocks and rerun the paged engine. How does the engine behave when memory runs out, and what happens to time to first token?
  2. Try block sizes 4, 16, and 64. How much memory goes to partly filled last blocks in each case?
  3. This engine preempts the newest request. Change it to preempt the request that holds the most blocks instead. Which choice finishes the workload in fewer steps, and which treats long requests more fairly?

Next

Section 5.4 lets requests that share a prompt prefix share its blocks too.

5.4 Prefix caching

Real traffic repeats itself. Requests to a chat product start with the same system prompt, and every turn of a conversation resends the turns before it. Those shared tokens get the same keys and values every time, because a token’s keys and values depend only on that token and the tokens before it. Prefix caching computes them once and shares their cache blocks between requests. With the paged cache from Section 5.3, it takes little code.

import logging
import time
import warnings
from collections import OrderedDict

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from max.driver import CPU
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.paged import BlockPool, PagedCache, PagedEngine
from minillm.step import Request, StepModel, compile_step
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)
step = compile_step(
    load_model(config, load_checkpoint(), DEVICE, StepModel), config, DEVICE
)

Naming blocks by their content

To share a block, the engine must recognize it. A full block is identified by the tokens it holds and every token before it, since those determine its keys and values. A chained hash captures both: each block’s hash combines the previous block’s hash with the block’s own tokens. Two requests share a block exactly when their prompts agree up to the end of that block.

The pool keeps a table from hash to block, counts how many sequences use each block, and frees a block only when no sequence uses it. A block with no users whose contents are still valid stays in the table, in least-recently-used order, so a later request can still reuse it. Such blocks are evicted only when the pool runs out of free blocks.

Only full blocks are shared, and new tokens always go into a sequence’s own next block, so a shared block is never written to.

from __future__ import annotations

from collections import OrderedDict

from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.paged import BlockPool, PagedCache, PagedEngine
from minillm.step import Request


class PrefixCachingPool(BlockPool):
    """A block pool that remembers full prompt blocks for reuse."""

    def __init__(
        self, config: LlamaConfig, num_blocks: int, block_size: int = 16
    ) -> None:
        super().__init__(config, num_blocks, block_size)
        self.refs = [0] * num_blocks
        self.by_hash: dict[int, int] = {}
        self.hash_of: dict[int, int] = {}
        self.unused: OrderedDict[int, None] = OrderedDict()  # cached, no users

    def allocate(self) -> int:
        if self.free:
            block = self.free.pop()
        elif self.unused:
            block, _ = self.unused.popitem(last=False)  # evict the oldest
            del self.by_hash[self.hash_of.pop(block)]
        else:
            raise MemoryError("the KV cache pool is full")
        self.refs[block] = 1
        return block

    def available(self) -> int:
        # Cached blocks that no request uses can be evicted and reused.
        return len(self.free) + len(self.unused)

    def release(self, blocks: list[int]) -> None:
        for block in blocks:
            self.refs[block] -= 1
            if self.refs[block] == 0:
                if block in self.hash_of:
                    self.unused[block] = None
                else:
                    self.free.append(block)

    def block_hashes(self, tokens: list[int]) -> list[int]:
        hashes, previous = [], 0
        for start in range(
            0, len(tokens) - self.block_size + 1, self.block_size
        ):
            previous = hash(
                (previous, tuple(tokens[start : start + self.block_size]))
            )
            hashes.append(previous)
        return hashes

    def lookup(self, prompt: list[int]) -> list[int]:
        """Returns the cached blocks that start ``prompt``, and claims them.
        The last prompt token is always left to compute, since its logits
        are needed."""
        blocks = []
        for h in self.block_hashes(prompt[:-1]):
            block = self.by_hash.get(h)
            if block is None:
                break
            self.refs[block] += 1
            self.unused.pop(block, None)
            blocks.append(block)
        return blocks

    def register(self, prompt: list[int], blocks: list[int]) -> None:
        """Records a sequence's full prompt blocks for later requests."""
        # The last block may be partial; only full blocks have hashes.
        for h, block in zip(self.block_hashes(prompt), blocks, strict=False):
            if h not in self.by_hash:
                self.by_hash[h] = block
                self.hash_of[block] = h


class PrefixCachingEngine(PagedEngine):
    """A paged engine that skips computing prompt prefixes it has cached."""

    def __init__(self, *args, pool: PrefixCachingPool, **kwargs) -> None:
        super().__init__(*args, pool=pool, **kwargs)
        self.computed_prompt_tokens = 0

    def prefill_tokens(self, request: Request, cache: PagedCache) -> list[int]:
        tokens = (
            request.prompt + request.output
        )  # output is empty unless preempted
        cache.blocks = self.pool.lookup(tokens)
        cache.length = len(cache.blocks) * self.pool.block_size
        new = tokens[cache.length :]
        self.computed_prompt_tokens += len(new)
        return new

    def prefilled(self, request: Request, cache: PagedCache) -> None:
        self.pool.register(request.prompt, cache.blocks)

Saved to minillm/prefix.py.

prefill_tokens and prefilled are the two hooks the engine from Section 5.2 calls around each prefill. The base engine returns the whole prompt from the first and does nothing in the second. The step model already handles the rest: a prefill with a non-empty past is a step with several new tokens.

A workload with a shared prefix

Eight users ask different questions of the same assistant. Its system prompt is about 150 tokens long; real ones are often much longer:

SYSTEM = (
    "You are a helpful assistant for a travel company. Answer briefly and politely. "
    "Recommend destinations in Europe, mention the best season to visit, give one "
    "practical tip about transport, and never invent prices. "
) * 4
QUESTIONS = [
    "Where should I go in spring?",
    "Is Rome good in winter?",
    "How do I get around Paris?",
    "What about Lisbon?",
    "Best city for museums?",
    "Where can I hike?",
    "Is Berlin expensive?",
    "Suggest a beach town.",
]


def workload() -> list[Request]:
    now = time.perf_counter()
    return [
        Request(tokenizer([SYSTEM + q])["input_ids"][0], 8, arrived=now)
        for q in QUESTIONS
    ]


print("prompt tokens per request:", len(workload()[0].prompt))


def serve(engine) -> list[Request]:
    requests = workload()
    for r in requests:
        engine.add(r)
    start = time.perf_counter()
    engine.run()
    ttft = [(r.first_token - r.arrived) * 1000 for r in requests]
    print(
        f"{type(engine).__name__:20} {time.perf_counter() - start:5.1f} s   "
        f"TTFT median {np.median(ttft):5.0f} ms, worst {max(ttft):5.0f} ms"
    )
    return requests


class CountingPrefixEngine(PrefixCachingEngine):
    """Records how many prompt tokens each request computes."""

    def prefill_tokens(self, request: Request, cache: object) -> list[int]:
        tokens = super().prefill_tokens(request, cache)
        self.computed_per_request[id(request)] = len(tokens)
        return tokens


plain = serve(
    PagedEngine(step, config, DEVICE, max_batch=4, pool=BlockPool(config, 128))
)
prefix_pool = PrefixCachingPool(config, 128)
cached_engine = CountingPrefixEngine(
    step, config, DEVICE, max_batch=4, pool=prefix_pool
)
cached_engine.computed_per_request = {}
cached = serve(cached_engine)

prompt tokens per request: 163
PagedEngine            6.4 s   TTFT median  2143 ms, worst  3812 ms
CountingPrefixEngine   5.6 s   TTFT median  1774 ms, worst  3106 ms

total_prompt = sum(len(r.prompt) for r in cached)
print(
    f"prompt tokens computed: {cached_engine.computed_prompt_tokens} of {total_prompt} "
    f"({cached_engine.computed_prompt_tokens / total_prompt:.0%})"
)
print("same tokens:", [r.output for r in cached] == [r.output for r in plain])
assert [r.output for r in cached] == [r.output for r in plain]
assert cached_engine.computed_prompt_tokens < total_prompt

computed = [cached_engine.computed_per_request[id(r)] for r in cached]
reused = [len(r.prompt) - c for r, c in zip(cached, computed)]
names = [f"request {i}: {q}" for i, q in enumerate(QUESTIONS)]
fig = go.Figure(
    [
        go.Bar(
            y=names,
            x=reused,
            name="reused from the cache",
            orientation="h",
            marker_color=viz.BLUE,
            hovertemplate="%{x} tokens reused<extra>%{y}</extra>",
        ),
        go.Bar(
            y=names,
            x=computed,
            name="computed",
            orientation="h",
            marker_color=viz.ORANGE,
            hovertemplate="%{x} tokens computed<extra>%{y}</extra>",
        ),
    ]
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Prompt tokens per request with prefix caching",
    barmode="stack",
    height=420,
    xaxis_title="prompt tokens",
    yaxis=dict(autorange="reversed", showgrid=False),
)
viz.show(fig)

prompt tokens computed: 715 of 1291 (55%)
same tokens: True

The first batch computes the system prompt once per request, because nothing is cached yet. From the second batch on, each request finds the system prompt’s full blocks in the table and computes only the rest: the system prompt’s last, partly filled block plus its own question. That brings the prompt tokens computed down to 715 of 1,291, or 55%. The outputs are identical, because the reused keys and values are exactly the ones the request would have computed.

The wall-clock gain is modest here: the whole workload took about 5.6 s with prefix caching against 6.4 s without, on this page’s machine (an Apple-silicon Mac with a Metal GPU), because prefilling about 160 prompt tokens on a 135M model is only part of the workload’s time. Treat these numbers as an example: yours depend on your hardware and vary from run to run. The saving grows with the shared prefix. A multi-turn chat resends the whole conversation so far on every turn, often thousands of tokens, and an agent resends long tool descriptions on every call, so for them prefix caching removes most of the prefill.

MAX Serve enables prefix caching by default; --no-enable-prefix-caching turns it off. The same idea extends across servers: a router can send each request to the replica that already holds its prefix. The handbook covers prefix caching and inference routing.

Exercises

  1. Send the workload twice through the same engine. What fraction of prompt tokens does the second pass compute?
  2. Shrink the pool so cached blocks must be evicted. Which requests lose their prefix, and why does least-recently-used eviction suit chat traffic?
  3. Why does lookup hash prompt[:-1] instead of the whole prompt? What would break otherwise?

Next

Section 5.5 speeds up decoding instead: speculative decoding can produce several tokens per step of the large model.

5.5 Speculative decoding

Decoding produces one token per step of the model, and at small batch sizes each step is memory-bound: Section 4.3 showed that a step for several tokens costs little more than a step for one. Speculative decoding uses that spare capacity. A small, fast draft model guesses the next few tokens, and the large target model checks all the guesses in one step. Each guess the target agrees with is an extra token from that one target step, and with greedy decoding the output is exactly what the target alone would produce.

This section implements it with SmolLM2-135M drafting for SmolLM2-360M, a larger model from the same family, and measures when it pays off.

import logging
import time
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from max.driver import CPU
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.step import ContiguousCache, StepModel, compile_step, run_step
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU
TARGET_ID = "HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M-Instruct"

Two changes to the step model

Verification needs the target’s prediction after every guessed token, not only the last, so the target’s step returns logits for all positions. When the target rejects a guess, the keys and values computed for the rejected tokens must be dropped, so the cache gets a truncate method.

class AllLogitsStepModel(StepModel):
    def forward(self, tokens, positions, mask, past):
        x = self.model.embed_tokens(tokens)
        new_kv = []
        for layer, layer_past in zip(self.model.layers, past, strict=True):
            x, kv = layer(x, positions, mask, layer_past)
            new_kv.append(kv)
        return self.lm_head(self.model.norm(x)), new_kv


class TruncatableCache(ContiguousCache):
    def truncate(self, length: int) -> None:
        for entry in self.layers:
            entry[0], entry[1] = entry[0][:length], entry[1][:length]


def build(model_id: str):
    config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained(model_id)
    model = load_model(
        config, load_checkpoint(model_id), DEVICE, AllLogitsStepModel
    )
    return config, compile_step(model, config, DEVICE)


draft_config, draft = build(MODEL_ID)
target_config, target = build(TARGET_ID)
print(
    f"draft: {draft_config.num_hidden_layers} layers x {draft_config.hidden_size}, "
    f"target: {target_config.num_hidden_layers} layers x {target_config.hidden_size}"
)

draft: 30 layers x 576, target: 32 layers x 960

Both models share a tokenizer, which this kind of speculative decoding requires: the target must read the draft’s token IDs as the same tokens.

The baseline: the target alone

tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)
prompt = tokenizer(["The three primary colors are"])["input_ids"][0]
N = 48


def target_alone() -> tuple[list[int], float]:
    cache, ids = TruncatableCache(target_config), list(prompt)
    start = time.perf_counter()
    logits = run_step(target, [cache], [ids], DEVICE)
    while True:
        ids.append(int(logits[0, -1].argmax()))
        if len(ids) - len(prompt) == N:
            break
        logits = run_step(target, [cache], [[ids[-1]]], DEVICE)
    return ids[len(prompt) :], time.perf_counter() - start


reference, reference_time = target_alone()
print(f"{N / reference_time:.1f} tokens/s")
print(tokenizer.decode(reference))

3.6 tokens/s
 red, blue, and yellow. These colors can be combined in various ways to create other colors. For example, by mixing red and blue, you get purple. Similarly, by mixing red and yellow, you get orange. By mixing blue

Draft, then verify

Each round:

  1. The draft model generates k tokens greedily, one step each. Its first step also feeds in any accepted tokens it hasn’t seen yet.
  2. The target runs one step over the last accepted token plus the k guesses, and returns its own greedy choice after each of them.
  3. Guesses are accepted from the left for as long as they match the target’s choice. At the first mismatch, the target’s choice replaces the guess. If all k match, the target’s choice after the last one comes free. So every round yields between 1 and k + 1 tokens.
  4. Both caches are truncated to the accepted tokens, which discards the keys and values of rejected guesses.

Because every emitted token is the target’s own greedy choice, the output is identical to the target alone. Sampling versions accept or reject each guess with a probability test that keeps the output distributed exactly as the target’s own samples would be.

def speculative(
    k: int, log: list | None = None
) -> tuple[list[int], float, int, int]:
    target_cache, draft_cache = (
        TruncatableCache(target_config),
        TruncatableCache(draft_config),
    )
    ids = list(prompt)
    rounds = accepted = 0
    start = time.perf_counter()
    logits = run_step(target, [target_cache], [ids], DEVICE)
    ids.append(int(logits[0, -1].argmax()))
    if log is not None:  # for the figure below: where each token came from
        log.append(("prefill", ids[-1]))
    while len(ids) - len(prompt) < N:
        guesses = []
        logits = run_step(
            draft, [draft_cache], [ids[draft_cache.length :]], DEVICE
        )
        for i in range(k):
            guesses.append(int(logits[0, -1].argmax()))
            if i < k - 1:
                logits = run_step(draft, [draft_cache], [[guesses[-1]]], DEVICE)
        checks = run_step(
            target,
            [target_cache],
            [ids[target_cache.length :] + guesses],
            DEVICE,
        )[0]
        choices = checks[-(k + 1) :].argmax(-1)
        n = 0
        while n < k and guesses[n] == int(choices[n]):
            n += 1
        ids += guesses[:n] + [int(choices[n])]
        if log is not None:
            log += [("accepted", t) for t in guesses[:n]]
            log.append(("bonus" if n == k else "corrected", int(choices[n])))
        rounds += 1
        accepted += n
        target_cache.truncate(len(ids) - 1)
        draft_cache.truncate(min(draft_cache.length, len(ids) - 1))
    return ids[len(prompt) :][:N], time.perf_counter() - start, rounds, accepted


print(
    f"{'k':>2} {'same output':>11} {'target steps':>12} {'accepted':>9} {'tokens/s':>9}"
)
print(f"{'-':>2} {'-':>11} {N:>12} {'-':>9} {N / reference_time:>9.1f}")
results = {}
for k in [2, 4, 6]:
    out, seconds, rounds, accepted = speculative(k)
    results[k] = (rounds, accepted)
    print(
        f"{k:>2} {str(out == reference):>11} {rounds:>12} {accepted / (rounds * k):>9.0%} {N / seconds:>9.1f}"
    )
    assert out == reference and rounds < N

 k same output target steps  accepted  tokens/s
 -           -           48         -       3.6
 2        True           21       64%       2.1
 4        True           15       58%       1.9
 6        True           15       41%       1.3

Look inside the run with k = 4. Each chip is one output token. The first, in yellow, comes from the target’s prefill. Blue chips are draft guesses the target accepted, orange ones are the target’s corrections where a guess was wrong, and green ones are the extra token a round earns when all four guesses are right. Hover over a chip for its round:

log = []
speculative(4, log)
log = log[:N]
kinds = {
    "prefill": viz.CHIP_COLORS[3],
    "accepted": viz.CHIP_COLORS[0],
    "corrected": viz.CHIP_COLORS[1],
    "bonus": viz.CHIP_COLORS[2],
}
titles, current = [], 0
for kind, _ in log:
    titles.append(
        "the target's prefill"
        if kind == "prefill"
        else f"round {current}: {kind}"
    )
    current += kind != "accepted"
viz.tokens(
    [tokenizer.decode([t]) for _, t in log],
    [t for _, t in log],
    colors=[kinds[kind] for kind, _ in log],
    titles=titles,
)
·red,·blue,·and·yellow.·These·colors·can·be·combined·in·various·ways·to·create·other·colors.·For·example,·by·mixing·red·and·blue,·you·get·purple.·Similarly,·by·mixing·red·and·yellow,·you·get·orange.·By·mixing·blue

The output matches the target alone, and the target runs far fewer steps: 15 to 21 verification rounds instead of 48 decode steps. Even so, on this page’s machine (an Apple-silicon Mac with a Metal GPU) every k was slower than the target alone, at about 1.3 to 2.1 tokens/s against about 3.6. Treat these numbers as an example: yours depend on your hardware and vary from run to run. A cost model explains why.

When it pays off

A round costs k draft steps plus one target step. If a draft step costs a fraction \(c\) of a target step, and a round yields \(\bar{n}\) tokens on average, the speedup over the target alone is

\[\text{speedup} \approx \frac{\bar{n}}{1 + k\,c}\]

This assumes the target verifies k + 1 tokens in about the time of a one-token step. Measure \(c\) for these two models:

def ms_per_decode_step(step, config) -> float:
    cache = TruncatableCache(config)
    run_step(step, [cache], [prompt], DEVICE)
    start = time.perf_counter()
    for _ in range(10):
        run_step(step, [cache], [[42]], DEVICE)
    return (time.perf_counter() - start) / 10 * 1000


c = ms_per_decode_step(draft, draft_config) / ms_per_decode_step(
    target, target_config
)
print(f"c = {c:.2f}")
for k, (rounds, accepted) in results.items():
    tokens_per_round = (accepted + rounds) / rounds
    print(
        f"k={k}: {tokens_per_round:.2f} tokens per round -> predicted speedup {tokens_per_round / (1 + k * c):.2f}x"
    )

c = 0.91
k=2: 2.29 tokens per round -> predicted speedup 0.81x
k=4: 3.33 tokens per round -> predicted speedup 0.72x
k=6: 3.47 tokens per round -> predicted speedup 0.54x

Plot the predicted speedup against the draft’s relative cost \(c\), using the acceptance measured for each k. Above the dashed line, speculation is faster than the target alone; the dotted line marks the \(c\) of these two models:

cs = np.logspace(-2, 0, 60)
fig = go.Figure()
for (k, (rounds, accepted)), color in zip(results.items(), viz.SERIES):
    per_round = (accepted + rounds) / rounds
    fig.add_trace(
        go.Scatter(
            x=cs,
            y=per_round / (1 + k * cs),
            name=f"k = {k}",
            line_color=color,
            hovertemplate=f"k = {k}, c = %{{x:.2f}}: %{{y:.2f}}x<extra></extra>",
        )
    )
fig.add_hline(y=1, line=dict(color=viz.INK_SECONDARY, dash="dash"))
fig.add_vline(
    x=c,
    line=dict(color=viz.INK_SECONDARY, dash="dot"),
    annotation_text=f"these models, c = {c:.2f}",
    annotation_position="top left",
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Predicted speedup of speculative decoding",
    height=420,
    xaxis=dict(
        title="draft step cost / target step cost, c (log scale)",
        type="log",
        dtick=1,
        range=[-2.05, 0.05],
    ),
    yaxis=dict(title="speedup over the target alone", rangemode="tozero"),
)
viz.show(fig)

Here a draft step costs about 0.9 of a target step, far more than the draft’s 2.7 times fewer parameters suggest. The two models have almost the same depth, 30 and 32 layers, and at this size much of each layer’s time is fixed per-step work that doesn’t shrink with the layer’s width. So the k draft steps in a round cost more than the target steps they save, and the cost model predicts speculation at about 0.5 to 0.8 times the speed of the target alone. The measured runs were slower still, about 0.4 to 0.6 times, but the conclusion is the same. A draft’s depth matters as much as its parameter count.

Speculative decoding pays off when the draft is cheap (small \(c\)), its guesses are usually accepted (large \(\bar{n}\)), and verifying k + 1 tokens costs about one target step. A cheap draft can be a 1B model drafting for a 70B target, or a small head trained on the target’s own hidden states, as in Medusa, EAGLE, and multi-token prediction (MTP). Cheap verification holds for memory-bound decoding on GPUs and less so on a busy CPU.

With \(c \approx 0.05\) and the acceptance measured above, the same code would run about two to three times as fast as the target alone, as the chart shows. MAX Serve’s speculative decoding uses drafts that read the target model’s hidden states, such as EAGLE and MTP, selected with --speculative-method. The handbook’s speculative decoding page compares the variants.

Exercises

  1. Plug different \(c\) values into the formula with the measured acceptance rates. For which \(c\) does each k break even?
  2. Change the prompt to something more predictable, such as counting from 1 to 50. How do acceptance and the best k change?
  3. Make k adaptive: increase it after a fully accepted round and decrease it after an early rejection. Does it help?

What you built

Chapter 5 is complete. Your engine schedules requests with continuous batching, stores keys and values in a paged cache, and shares cached prefixes between requests, and a separate loop speculates with a draft model for a single sequence. MAX Serve is built from the same parts, with the kernels and scheduling that make them fast on GPUs. Chapter 6 returns to the model and extends it to newer architectures.

6. Bigger models

Your SmolLM2 is a plain Llama. Newer models change a few parts, each for a reason. This chapter extends your model to them, and everything still runs on a laptop.

SectionYou learn
