Related handbook pages: choosing a model, quantization, and parallelism strategies.

6.1 Qwen3: normalized queries and keys

Most open models are close relatives of the Llama architecture you built in Chapter 1. To bring up a new one, you find what changed and why. This section adds Qwen3, here the 0.6-billion-parameter model Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B. It subclasses your Llama classes, overrides only what differs, and verifies the result with the tools from Chapter 4.

import logging
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
import torch
from max.driver import CPU
from max.dtype import DType
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer
from max.tensor import Tensor
from max.tensor.tensor import TensorType
from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.sampling import generate
from minillm.testing import compare_outputs
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU
QWEN3 = "Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B"

What changed

Compare the two configs:

smol, qwen = (
    LlamaConfig.from_pretrained(MODEL_ID),
    LlamaConfig.from_pretrained(QWEN3),
)
for field in [
    "vocab_size",
    "hidden_size",
    "num_hidden_layers",
    "num_attention_heads",
    "num_key_value_heads",
    "head_dim",
    "intermediate_size",
    "rope_theta",
    "rms_norm_eps",
]:
    print(f"{field:22} {getattr(smol, field):>10} {getattr(qwen, field):>10}")

vocab_size                  49152     151936
hidden_size                   576       1024
num_hidden_layers              30         28
num_attention_heads             9         16
num_key_value_heads             3          8
head_dim                       64        128
intermediate_size            1536       3072
rope_theta                 100000    1000000
rms_norm_eps                1e-05      1e-06

Four differences matter:

  • A larger vocabulary, 151,936 tokens, which covers many languages and code. The embedding alone holds 156M parameters, about a quarter of the model.
  • An explicit head_dim: 16 heads of 128 dimensions make 2048, wider than the 1024-dimension hidden state. q_proj projects up, and o_proj projects back down. The config class from Section 1.3 reads head_dim when a model sets it.
  • A larger RoPE base, 1,000,000, so the rotations turn more slowly and positions stay distinguishable over longer contexts.
  • QK-norm: an RMSNorm on every query and key head, before RoPE.

The first three are config values that your code already handles. QK-norm needs new code.

QK-norm

Attention scores are dot products of queries and keys. During training, nothing stops some heads’ queries and keys from growing until a few scores dominate every softmax, which can destabilize the training of large models. Normalizing the queries and keys, with a learned scale per dimension, bounds the scores. Qwen3 and Gemma 3 normalize each head separately; OLMo 2 normalizes the whole query and key projections.

For each head’s query \(q\) (and likewise each key), with \(d\) = head_dim and a learned weight \(w\) of size \(d\):

\[\hat{q} = \frac{q}{\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{d}\sum_j q_j^2 + \epsilon}} \odot w\]

The division gives every query the same root mean square, so the length of \(\hat q\) depends on \(w\) and on \(q\)’s direction, not on \(q\)’s size. A score \(\hat q \cdot \hat k\) is then bounded however large \(q\) and \(k\) get. In code, QK-norm is two RMSNorms of size head_dim inside attention, applied to each head after the projection and before RoPE. The other classes only swap in the new attention:

from __future__ import annotations

from max.graph import Dim
from max.nn import ModuleList, RMSNorm
from max.tensor import Tensor

from minillm.attention import Attention
from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.layers import apply_rope, rope_cos_sin
from minillm.model import DecoderLayer, LlamaForCausalLM, LlamaModel


class Qwen3Attention(Attention):
    """Llama attention with an RMSNorm on each query and key head."""

    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.q_norm = RMSNorm(self.head_dim, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)
        self.k_norm = RMSNorm(self.head_dim, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)

    def project(
        self, x: Tensor, start: int | Dim | Tensor
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, Tensor, Tensor]:
        batch, seq, _ = x.shape
        q = self.q_proj(x).reshape([batch, seq, self.num_heads, self.head_dim])
        k = self.k_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, seq, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        v = self.v_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, seq, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        q, k = self.q_norm(q), self.k_norm(k)
        cos, sin = rope_cos_sin(
            self.head_dim, self.rope_theta, start, seq, x.mesh
        )
        return apply_rope(q, cos, sin), apply_rope(k, cos, sin), v


class Qwen3DecoderLayer(DecoderLayer):
    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.self_attn = Qwen3Attention(config)


class Qwen3Model(LlamaModel):
    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.layers = ModuleList(
            [Qwen3DecoderLayer(config) for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)]
        )


class Qwen3ForCausalLM(LlamaForCausalLM):
    """Qwen3: a Llama model whose attention normalizes queries and keys."""

    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.model = Qwen3Model(config)

Saved to minillm/qwen3.py.

The new parameters are named q_norm and k_norm, as in Qwen3’s checkpoint, so load_model from Section 1.6 loads it unchanged. load_model builds the model inside F.lazy(), so the Llama layers that the subclasses replace are never allocated.

model = load_model(qwen, load_checkpoint(QWEN3), DEVICE, Qwen3ForCausalLM)
compiled = model.compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
)
n_params = sum(p.num_elements() for _, p in model.parameters)
print(
    f"{n_params / 1e6:.0f}M values, including the untied copy of the embedding"
)

752M values, including the untied copy of the embedding

To see what QK-norm guarantees on real activations, run layer 0’s input norm and query projection on a sentence. Then scale the queries up 10 times, as projection weights that grew during training would. The figure compares the median length of the query vectors in each head, before and after q_norm:

attn = model.model.layers[0].self_attn
ids = AutoTokenizer(QWEN3)(
    ["Normalizing queries and keys keeps attention scores in a stable range."]
)["input_ids"][0]
h = model.model.layers[0].input_layernorm(
    model.model.embed_tokens(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE))
)
q = attn.q_proj(h).reshape([1, len(ids), attn.num_heads, attn.head_dim])
styles = {}
for scale, dash in [(1, "solid"), (10, "dash")]:
    scaled = q * scale
    styles[f"before q_norm, input x{scale}"] = (scaled, viz.ORANGE, dash)
    styles[f"after q_norm, input x{scale}"] = (
        attn.q_norm(scaled),
        viz.BLUE,
        dash,
    )

fig = go.Figure()
for name, (t, color, dash) in styles.items():
    median = np.median(
        np.linalg.norm(t.to_numpy()[0], axis=-1), axis=0
    )  # per head
    fig.add_trace(
        go.Scatter(
            x=np.arange(attn.num_heads),
            y=median,
            name=name,
            mode="lines+markers",
            line=dict(
                color=color, dash=dash, width=4 if dash == "solid" else 2
            ),
            hovertemplate="head %{x}: %{y:.1f}<extra>" + name + "</extra>",
        )
    )
fig.update_layout(
    title="Median query vector length per head, Qwen3 layer 0",
    height=440,
    xaxis=dict(title="query head", dtick=1),
    yaxis=dict(title="length (log scale)", type="log", dtick=1),
)
viz.show(fig)

Without the norm, query lengths grow with the queries: queries 10 times larger are 10 times longer, and scores grow 100 times once the keys grow too. After q_norm, the two blue lines lie on top of each other. Each query’s length now depends only on its direction and the learned per-dimension scale, which also gives each head its own typical size, so no input can make a score blow up.

Verify it

Compare with Hugging Face’s PyTorch Qwen3 on a few prompts, using the metrics from Section 4.2:

tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(QWEN3)
reference = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
    QWEN3, torch_dtype=torch.float32
).eval()


def softmax(z: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    e = np.exp(z.astype(np.float64) - z.max(-1, keepdims=True))
    return e / e.sum(-1, keepdims=True)


for text in [
    "The capital of France is",
    "def add(a, b):\n    return",
    "Le chat est assis sur",
]:
    ids = tokenizer([text])["input_ids"][0]
    ours = compiled(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()[0]
    with torch.no_grad():
        theirs = reference(torch.tensor([ids])).logits[0].numpy()
    r = compare_outputs(ours, theirs, atol=1e-3, rtol=1e-3)
    p, q = softmax(theirs), softmax(ours)
    kl = float((p * (np.log(p + 1e-30) - np.log(q + 1e-30))).sum(-1).mean())
    top1 = (ours.argmax(-1) == theirs.argmax(-1)).mean()
    print(
        f"{text[:24]!r:28} max err {r.max_abs_err:.1e}  KL {kl:.1e}  top-1 {top1:.0%}"
    )
    assert top1 >= 0.99 and kl < 1e-6

'The capital of France is'   max err 1.4e-04  KL 1.0e-10  top-1 100%
'def add(a, b):\n    retur'  max err 1.1e-04  KL 1.9e-11  top-1 100%
'Le chat est assis sur'      max err 8.5e-05  KL 7.8e-11  top-1 100%

def next_logits(ids: list[int]) -> Tensor:
    return compiled(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE))[0, -1]


prompt = tokenizer(["The three primary colors are"])["input_ids"][0]
print(
    tokenizer.decode(prompt + generate(next_logits, prompt, 24, temperature=0))
)

The three primary colors are red, blue, and yellow. The three secondary colors are red and blue, blue and yellow, and red and yellow

That is the whole bring-up: about 40 lines of model code for a new architecture, checked against the reference to rounding error. MAX’s own Qwen3 implementation serves the same model with a paged KV cache and continuous batching, the techniques that Chapter 5 builds:

max serve --model Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B --quantization-encoding float32 --no-prefer-module-v3

--no-prefer-module-v3 selects MAX’s text-generation implementation of Qwen3. Without it, current nightly builds pick Qwen3’s embedding variant for this checkpoint and fail to start.

Qwen3 is also a reasoning model: by default it thinks step by step inside <think> tags before it answers, which costs tokens and improves answers to hard questions. Its chat template has an enable_thinking switch that turns this off. The handbook’s choosing the right model page discusses that trade-off.

Exercises

  1. Add a KV cache to Qwen3: subclass CachedAttention from Section 1.8 with the same q_norm and k_norm, and check it against the full model.
  2. Remove QK-norm: copy load_model from Section 1.6, build LlamaForCausalLM instead of Qwen3ForCausalLM, and pass strict=False to load_state_dict so that it skips the q_norm and k_norm weights. Rerun the comparison. What happens to the output?
  3. Count the parameters in the embedding, the attention projections, and the MLPs. How do the proportions compare with SmolLM2’s?

Next

Section 6.2 adds sliding-window attention with Gemma 3, and Section 6.3 builds a mixture of experts.

6.2 Gemma 3: sliding-window attention

Attention’s cost grows with the square of the context, and the KV cache grows linearly with it. Gemma 3 reduces both with sliding-window attention: most of its layers look only at the most recent 512 tokens, and every sixth layer looks at all of them. This section brings up Gemma 3 270M, which differs from Llama in more places than Qwen3 did in Section 6.1, and verifies it on a prompt long enough for the window to matter.

Gemma’s weights are gated: accept the license on the model page and make a Hugging Face token available (on Colab, as the HF_TOKEN secret).

import logging
import math
import warnings
from dataclasses import dataclass

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
import torch
from max import functional as F
from max.driver import CPU
from max.dtype import DType
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer
from max.tensor import Tensor
from max.tensor.tensor import TensorType
from plotly.subplots import make_subplots
from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.testing import compare_outputs
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU
GEMMA = "google/gemma-3-270m-it"

What changed

Gemma 3’s config.json has fields that Llama’s doesn’t. A config subclass adds them, and from_pretrained reads every dataclass field it finds:

from __future__ import annotations

import math
from dataclasses import dataclass, replace

from max import functional as F
from max.driver import Device
from max.dtype import DType
from max.graph import Dim
from max.nn import Embedding, GemmaRMSNorm, Linear, Module, ModuleList
from max.nn.kernels.attention import flash_attention
from max.tensor import Tensor
from max.tensor.sharding import DeviceMesh

from minillm.attention import Attention, causal_mask
from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.layers import MLP, apply_rope, rope_cos_sin


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Gemma3Config(LlamaConfig):
    sliding_window: int = 512
    layer_types: tuple[str, ...] = ()
    rope_local_base_freq: float = 10000.0
    query_pre_attn_scalar: float = 256.0

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        super().__post_init__()
        object.__setattr__(self, "layer_types", tuple(self.layer_types))

Saved to minillm/gemma3.py.

config = Gemma3Config.from_pretrained(GEMMA)
print(config)
print(
    "global layers:",
    [i for i, t in enumerate(config.layer_types) if t == "full_attention"],
)

Gemma3Config(vocab_size=262144, hidden_size=640, intermediate_size=2048, num_hidden_layers=18, num_attention_heads=4, num_key_value_heads=1, rope_theta=1000000.0, rms_norm_eps=1e-06, max_position_embeddings=32768, tie_word_embeddings=True, eos_token_id=1, head_dim=256, sliding_window=512, layer_types=('sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'full_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'full_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'sliding_attention', 'full_attention'), rope_local_base_freq=10000.0, query_pre_attn_scalar=256)
global layers: [5, 11, 17]

Compared with your Llama, Gemma 3 changes six things:

PartLlamaGemma 3
Attention spanevery layer sees all past tokens5 of 6 layers see only the last 512
RoPE baseone, 100,000 for SmolLM210,000 for windowed layers, 1,000,000 for global ones
Norms per layer2, before attention and before the MLP4, before and after each
RMSNormx / rms(x) * wx / rms(x) * (1 + w) (max.nn.GemmaRMSNorm)
MLP activationSiLU (SwiGLU)tanh-approximated GELU (GeGLU)
Embeddingas storedmultiplied by \(\sqrt{\text{hidden size}}\)

It also has QK-norm, as in Qwen3, and scales attention scores by one over the square root of query_pre_attn_scalar instead of the head dimension (both are 256 in this model). Its vocabulary has 262,144 tokens, so the embedding holds 168M of the model’s 270M parameters.

Sliding-window attention

A windowed layer adds one more condition to the causal mask: query i may see key j only if \(i - j < 512\). F.band_part builds that band. Each layer reads its own RoPE base and window from the config.

The mask uses a large finite number instead of \(-\infty\), for the reason Section 5.1 gave: a query more than 512 tokens in has its whole first block of keys hidden, and a blockwise attention kernel computes \(e^{-\infty + \infty}\) = NaN for such a row.

def sliding_window_mask(
    q_len: int | Dim,
    kv_len: int | Dim,
    window: int,
    device: Device | DeviceMesh,
) -> Tensor:
    """Like ``causal_mask``, but each query also can't see keys ``window`` or
    more positions behind it."""
    # A finite value, not -inf: a window hides a query's whole first block of
    # keys, and a blockwise attention kernel would turn that into NaN.
    blocked = F.broadcast_to(
        Tensor(-1e30, dtype=DType.float32, device=device), [q_len, kv_len]
    )
    return F.band_part(
        blocked, num_lower=window - 1, num_upper=kv_len - q_len, exclude=True
    )


class Gemma3Attention(Attention):
    def __init__(self, config: Gemma3Config, layer: int) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.sliding = config.layer_types[layer] == "sliding_attention"
        self.window = config.sliding_window
        self.rope_theta = (
            config.rope_local_base_freq if self.sliding else config.rope_theta
        )
        self.scale = config.query_pre_attn_scalar**-0.5
        self.q_norm = GemmaRMSNorm(self.head_dim, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)
        self.k_norm = GemmaRMSNorm(self.head_dim, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)

    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        batch, seq, _ = x.shape
        q = self.q_norm(
            self.q_proj(x).reshape([batch, seq, self.num_heads, self.head_dim])
        )
        k = self.k_norm(
            self.k_proj(x).reshape(
                [batch, seq, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
            )
        )
        v = self.v_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, seq, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        cos, sin = rope_cos_sin(self.head_dim, self.rope_theta, 0, seq, x.mesh)
        q, k = apply_rope(q, cos, sin), apply_rope(k, cos, sin)
        if self.sliding:
            mask = sliding_window_mask(seq, seq, self.window, x.mesh)
        else:
            mask = causal_mask(seq, seq, x.mesh)
        mask = F.broadcast_to(mask, [batch, seq, seq])
        out = flash_attention(q, k, v, self.scale, mask).rebind_mapping(
            q.mapping
        )
        return self.o_proj(
            out.reshape([batch, seq, self.num_heads * self.head_dim])
        )

Saved to minillm/gemma3.py.

Compare the two masks on 16 tokens, with a window small enough to see. The slider changes the window; Gemma 3’s is 512:

seq = 16
full = (causal_mask(seq, seq, DEVICE).to_numpy() == 0).astype(int)
steps = []
for window in [2, 4, 8, 16]:

        sliding_window_mask(seq, seq, window, DEVICE).to_numpy() == 0
    ).astype(int)
    steps.append(
        [
            go.Heatmap(

                + "%{z}"

fig = make_subplots(
    rows=1,
    cols=2,

        "global layer: causal",

)

    title_text="query position", showgrid=False, autorange="reversed"
)

)

Once the sequence is longer than the window, a sliding layer’s cost per query stops growing, and so does the KV cache it needs: only the last window keys. With five sliding layers for every global one, most of Gemma 3’s KV cache stops growing at 512 tokens.

The rest of the model

GeGLU is the SwiGLU MLP with a different activation. The decoder layer has the four norms, and the model scales its embedding:

class GeGLU(MLP):
    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        return self.down_proj(
            F.gelu(self.gate_proj(x), approximate="tanh") * self.up_proj(x)
        )


class Gemma3DecoderLayer(Module):
    def __init__(self, config: Gemma3Config, layer: int) -> None:
        eps = config.rms_norm_eps
        self.input_layernorm = GemmaRMSNorm(config.hidden_size, eps=eps)
        self.self_attn = Gemma3Attention(config, layer)
        self.post_attention_layernorm = GemmaRMSNorm(
            config.hidden_size, eps=eps
        )
        self.pre_feedforward_layernorm = GemmaRMSNorm(
            config.hidden_size, eps=eps
        )
        self.mlp = GeGLU(config)
        self.post_feedforward_layernorm = GemmaRMSNorm(
            config.hidden_size, eps=eps
        )

    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        x = x + self.post_attention_layernorm(
            self.self_attn(self.input_layernorm(x))
        )
        return x + self.post_feedforward_layernorm(
            self.mlp(self.pre_feedforward_layernorm(x))
        )


class Gemma3Model(Module):
    def __init__(self, config: Gemma3Config) -> None:
        self.embed_scale = math.sqrt(config.hidden_size)
        self.embed_tokens = Embedding(config.vocab_size, dim=config.hidden_size)
        self.layers = ModuleList(
            [
                Gemma3DecoderLayer(config, i)
                for i in range(config.num_hidden_layers)
            ]
        )
        self.norm = GemmaRMSNorm(config.hidden_size, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)

    def forward(self, input_ids: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        x = self.embed_tokens(input_ids) * self.embed_scale
        for layer in self.layers:
            x = layer(x)
        return self.norm(x)


class Gemma3ForCausalLM(Module):
    def __init__(self, config: Gemma3Config) -> None:
        self.model = Gemma3Model(config)
        self.lm_head = Linear(config.hidden_size, config.vocab_size, bias=False)

    def forward(self, input_ids: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        return self.lm_head(self.model(input_ids))


def without_sliding_window(config: Gemma3Config) -> Gemma3Config:
    """Returns ``config`` with windows too wide to hide any token; each layer
    keeps its own RoPE base."""
    return replace(config, sliding_window=config.max_position_embeddings)

Saved to minillm/gemma3.py.

weights = load_checkpoint(GEMMA)
model = load_model(config, weights, DEVICE, Gemma3ForCausalLM)
compiled = model.compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
)

Verify it

A short prompt never reaches the window, so verify on two prompts: a short one, and one of about 700 tokens, where windowed layers must ignore the oldest tokens.

tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(GEMMA)
reference = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
    GEMMA, torch_dtype=torch.float32, attn_implementation="eager"
).eval()
prompts = {
    "short": "The capital of France is",
    "long (700 tokens)": "The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. " * 70
    + "The capital of France is",
}


def softmax(z: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    e = np.exp(z.astype(np.float64) - z.max(-1, keepdims=True))
    return e / e.sum(-1, keepdims=True)


def verify(run) -> dict[str, tuple[float, float]]:  # noqa: ANN001
    # Prints and returns each prompt's KL divergence and top-1 agreement.
    results = {}
    for name, text in prompts.items():
        ids = tokenizer([text])["input_ids"][0]
        ours = run(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()[0]
        with torch.no_grad():
            theirs = reference(torch.tensor([ids])).logits[0].numpy()
        r = compare_outputs(ours, theirs)
        p, q = softmax(theirs), softmax(ours)
        kl = float((p * (np.log(p + 1e-30) - np.log(q + 1e-30))).sum(-1).mean())
        top1 = (ours.argmax(-1) == theirs.argmax(-1)).mean()
        print(
            f"{name:18} {len(ids):4} tokens  max err {r.max_abs_err:.1e}  KL {kl:.1e}  top-1 {top1:.0%}"
        )
        results[name] = (kl, top1)
    return results


matched = verify(compiled)
assert all(kl < 1e-6 and top1 >= 0.99 for kl, top1 in matched.values())

short                 6 tokens  max err 1.6e-04  KL 4.8e-11  top-1 100%
long (700 tokens)   706 tokens  max err 4.1e-04  KL 6.5e-12  top-1 100%

Both match. To see that the window does real work, build the same model with windows too wide to hide anything, keep each layer’s RoPE base, and verify again:

no_window = load_model(
    without_sliding_window(config), weights, DEVICE, Gemma3ForCausalLM
)
unwindowed = verify(
    no_window.compile(
        TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
    )
)
assert unwindowed["short"][0] < 1e-6  # no query is far enough back to notice
assert (
    unwindowed["long (700 tokens)"][0] > 100 * matched["long (700 tokens)"][0]
)

short                 6 tokens  max err 1.6e-04  KL 4.8e-11  top-1 100%
long (700 tokens)   706 tokens  max err 4.9e+01  KL 2.8e-01  top-1 93%

On the short prompt the two models are identical, because no query is more than 512 tokens from any key. On the long prompt the model without the window diverges from the reference. The windowed layers do ignore the oldest tokens, and the bring-up must get the window right to match.

A windowed layer’s attention cost and KV cache stop growing at 512 tokens. With 15 of Gemma 3’s 18 layers windowed, a 32,000-token context needs about a fifth of the cache that a fully global model would need. The handbook’s GPU memory calculation page shows how cache size limits context and batch size.

Exercises

  3. Check the embedding scale: remove embed_scale and verify again. Which metric shows it?

Next

Section 6.3 builds a mixture-of-experts layer and spreads its experts across devices.

6.3 Mixture of experts

The largest open models are mixtures of experts (MoE). An MoE layer has many MLPs, the experts, instead of one, and a small router sends each token to only a few of them. The model holds the parameters of all its experts, while each token pays for only a fraction of them. This section builds an MoE layer, checks it against Hugging Face’s Qwen3-MoE implementation, and then spreads the experts across devices.

No real MoE checkpoint fits on a laptop, so this section uses Qwen3-MoE’s architecture at a small size with random weights, a common way to test large-model code. As in Section 4.1, the test compares one layer with a reference implementation.

import json

    DeviceMapping,
    DeviceMesh,

    Sharded,
    auto_reshard,
)
from plotly.subplots import make_subplots
from transformers import AutoConfig
from transformers.models.qwen3_moe.modeling_qwen3_moe import (
    Qwen3MoeSparseMoeBlock,
)

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

How big, and how sparse

Qwen3-30B-A3B is a typical MoE model. Its name gives its total parameters, 30 billion, and its active ones, about 3 billion:

real = json.loads(
    Path(hf_hub_download("Qwen/Qwen3-30B-A3B", "config.json")).read_text()
)
E, K, H, I = (
    real["num_experts"],
    real["num_experts_per_tok"],
    real["hidden_size"],
    real["moe_intermediate_size"],
)
per_expert = 3 * H * I
print(f"{E} experts per layer, {K} chosen per token")
print(
    f"expert weights per layer: {E * per_expert / 1e6:.0f}M, used per token: {K * per_expert / 1e6:.0f}M"
)

128 experts per layer, 8 chosen per token
expert weights per layer: 604M, used per token: 38M

Every token uses 8 of the 128 experts, so it touches only a sixteenth of each layer’s expert weights. Adding experts adds parameters without adding work per token. The cost moves to memory, since every expert must be loaded somewhere, which is why MoE models are usually served across several GPUs. The handbook’s parallelism strategies page covers expert parallelism, which the end of this section implements.

The layer

An MoE layer replaces the MLP. For each token:

  1. The router, a Linear from the hidden size to the number of experts, scores every expert. Softmax turns the scores into probabilities.
  2. The K most probable experts are chosen, and their probabilities are renormalized to sum to 1.
  3. Each chosen expert, a SwiGLU MLP like the one in Section 1.4, processes the token, and the outputs are summed, weighted by those probabilities.

In symbols, with router weights \(W_r\), the set \(\mathcal{T}\) of the \(K\) most probable experts, and expert \(e\)’s MLP \(E_e\):

\[p = \mathrm{softmax}(W_r x), \qquad y = \sum_{e \in \mathcal{T}} \frac{p_e}{\sum_{j \in \mathcal{T}} p_j} \, E_e(x)\]

The experts’ weights are stored stacked, with the expert as the first axis: one tensor for the fused gate and up projections, and one for the down projection. That is the layout of Hugging Face’s Qwen3-MoE module; the checkpoint files store each expert’s projections separately. This version computes every expert on every token and multiplies the unselected ones by zero. That wastes work, but it is simple and exact, and the last part of this section shows that it is easy to distribute.

from __future__ import annotations

from max import functional as F
from max.dtype import DType
from max.nn import Linear, Module
from max.tensor import Tensor


class Experts(Module):
    """The stacked weights of every expert: gate and up projections fused."""

    def __init__(
        self, num_experts: int, hidden: int, intermediate: int
    ) -> None:
        self.gate_up_proj = Tensor.zeros(
            [num_experts, 2 * intermediate, hidden]
        )
        self.down_proj = Tensor.zeros([num_experts, hidden, intermediate])


class MoE(Module):
    """A mixture-of-experts layer with top-k routing, as in Qwen3-MoE."""

    def __init__(
        self, num_experts: int, top_k: int, hidden: int, intermediate: int
    ) -> None:
        self.num_experts, self.top_k, self.intermediate = (
            num_experts,
            top_k,
            intermediate,
        )
        self.gate = Linear(hidden, num_experts, bias=False)  # the router
        self.experts = Experts(num_experts, hidden, intermediate)

    def routing(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        """Returns each token's weight for each expert, ``[tokens, experts]``,
        zero for experts the token wasn't routed to."""
        tokens = x.shape[0]
        weights, chosen = F.top_k(F.softmax(self.gate(x)), self.top_k)
        weights = weights / weights.sum(axis=-1)
        ids = Tensor.arange(self.num_experts, dtype=DType.int64, device=x.mesh)
        one_hot = (
            chosen.reshape([tokens, self.top_k, 1])
            == ids.reshape([1, 1, self.num_experts])
        ).cast(x.dtype)
        return (
            (one_hot * weights.reshape([tokens, self.top_k, 1]))
            .sum(axis=1)
            .reshape([tokens, self.num_experts])
        )

    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        """``x`` is ``[tokens, hidden]``."""
        tokens, hidden = x.shape
        gate_up = x.reshape(
            [1, tokens, hidden]
        ) @ self.experts.gate_up_proj.transpose(1, 2)
        gate, up = (
            gate_up[:, :, : self.intermediate],
            gate_up[:, :, self.intermediate :],
        )
        outputs = (F.silu(gate) * up) @ self.experts.down_proj.transpose(
            1, 2
        )  # [E, T, H]
        weights = self.routing(x).T.reshape([self.num_experts, tokens, 1])
        return (weights * outputs).sum(axis=0).reshape([tokens, hidden])

Saved to minillm/moe.py.

Check it against Qwen3-MoE

Build Hugging Face’s Qwen3-MoE block at a small size, 8 experts with 2 per token, fill it with random weights, and load the same weights into yours. The parameter names match, so load_state_dict takes the PyTorch state directly:

torch.manual_seed(0)
small = AutoConfig.from_pretrained("Qwen/Qwen3-30B-A3B")
(
    small.hidden_size,
    small.moe_intermediate_size,
    small.num_experts,
    small.num_experts_per_tok,
) = 64, 32, 8, 2
reference = Qwen3MoeSparseMoeBlock(small).float().eval()
for p in reference.parameters():
    torch.nn.init.normal_(p, std=0.2)
state = {name: p.detach().numpy() for name, p in reference.state_dict().items()}
print({name: w.shape for name, w in state.items()})

with default_device(DEVICE), default_dtype(DType.float32):
    moe = MoE(num_experts=8, top_k=2, hidden=64, intermediate=32)
moe.load_state_dict(state)

x = np.random.default_rng(0).standard_normal((10, 64)).astype(np.float32)
with torch.no_grad():
    expected = reference(torch.from_numpy(x)[None])[0].numpy()
ours = moe(Tensor(x, device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()
print(f"max difference {np.abs(ours - expected).max():.1e}")
assert np.abs(ours - expected).max() < 1e-4

{'experts.gate_up_proj': (8, 64, 64), 'experts.down_proj': (8, 64, 32), 'gate.weight': (8, 64)}
max difference 3.8e-06

Where the tokens go

The routing matrix shows which experts each token uses. Count the tokens per expert for a larger batch:

batch = Tensor(
    np.random.default_rng(1).standard_normal((512, 64)).astype(np.float32),
    device=DEVICE,
)
load = (moe.routing(batch).to_numpy() > 0).sum(axis=0)
print("tokens per expert:", load, " ideal:", 512 * 2 // 8)
assert load.sum() == 512 * 2

weights_np = moe.routing(batch).to_numpy()
fig = make_subplots(
    rows=1,
    cols=2,
    column_widths=[0.6, 0.4],
    horizontal_spacing=0.2,
    subplot_titles=[
        "Routing weights of the first 32 tokens",
        "Tokens per expert, all 512",
    ],
)
fig.add_trace(
    go.Heatmap(
        z=np.where(weights_np[:32] > 0, weights_np[:32], np.nan),
        colorscale=viz.SEQUENTIAL,
        zmin=0,
        zmax=1,
        xgap=2,
        ygap=2,
        colorbar=dict(title="weight", thickness=12, x=0.5),
        hovertemplate="token %{y} -> expert %{x}: weight %{z:.2f}<extra></extra>",
    ),
    row=1,
    col=1,
)
fig.add_trace(
    go.Bar(
        x=np.arange(len(load)),
        y=load,
        marker_color=viz.BLUE,
        showlegend=False,
        hovertemplate="expert %{x}: %{y} tokens<extra></extra>",
    ),
    row=1,
    col=2,
)
fig.add_hline(
    y=512 * 2 / len(load),
    line=dict(color=viz.INK_SECONDARY, dash="dash"),
    row=1,
    col=2,
    annotation_text="even split",
    annotation_position="top right",
)
fig.update_xaxes(title_text="expert", dtick=1, showgrid=False)
fig.update_yaxes(
    title_text="token", autorange="reversed", showgrid=False, row=1, col=1
)
fig.update_yaxes(title_text="tokens routed", row=1, col=2)
fig.update_layout(title="Top-2 routing over 8 experts", height=480)
viz.show(fig)

tokens per expert: [122 116 157 121 117 134 122 135]  ideal: 128

Every token went to exactly two experts. How evenly a batch spreads across experts matters in practice: an expert that gets far more tokens than the others becomes the slowest part of the layer. MoE models are usually trained with a load-balancing term in the loss that pushes the router to spread tokens evenly, and serving systems monitor the load on each expert.

This dense version computes all 8 experts for every token, 4 times the work that the 2 chosen experts need. Production kernels instead group the tokens by expert and run one matrix multiplication per expert over only its tokens (a grouped GEMM), which removes the waste:

per_expert_small = 3 * 64 * 32  # gate, up, and down projections
dense = 2 * 512 * 8 * per_expert_small
sparse = 2 * 512 * 2 * per_expert_small
print(
    f"dense: {dense / 1e6:.1f} MFLOP, routed: {sparse / 1e6:.1f} MFLOP, {dense / sparse:.0f}x less"
)

dense: 50.3 MFLOP, routed: 12.6 MFLOP, 4x less

Expert parallelism

The experts are independent, so they can live on different devices. With 8 experts on 4 devices, each device holds 2. Every device receives every token, runs its own experts, and weights their outputs; the final sum over experts spans devices, which takes one all-reduce. On the simulated mesh from Section 2.1, the only change is to shard the stacked expert weights along the expert axis:

mesh = DeviceMesh((CPU(),) * 4, (4,), ("ep",))
with default_device(mesh), default_dtype(DType.float32):
    ep_moe = MoE(num_experts=8, top_k=2, hidden=64, intermediate=32)
by_expert = DeviceMapping(mesh, (Sharded(0),))
ep_moe.experts.gate_up_proj = ep_moe.experts.gate_up_proj.to(by_expert)
ep_moe.experts.down_proj = ep_moe.experts.down_proj.to(by_expert)
ep_moe.load_state_dict(state)
print(ep_moe.experts.down_proj.mapping)

with warnings.catch_warnings(record=True) as caught:
    warnings.simplefilter("always")
    with auto_reshard(mode="warn"):
        ep_out = ep_moe(Tensor(x, device=DeviceMapping(mesh, (Replicated(),))))
for w in caught:
    if "reshards" in str(w.message):
        print(w.message)
print(
    f"max difference from the reference: {np.abs(ep_out.to_numpy() - expected).max():.1e}"
)
assert np.abs(ep_out.to_numpy() - expected).max() < 1e-4

DeviceMapping(DeviceMesh(ep=4), [Sharded(axis=0)])

mul(lhs, rhs) reshards lhs
  lhs    : float32[8, 10, 1]@mesh(ep=4){"ep":R}
  rhs    : float32[8, 10, 64]@mesh(ep=4){"ep":S0}
  region : auto_reshard(DEFAULT_TRANSITIONS, mode="warn")

  decided per mesh axis
  axis    lhs           rhs           -> out        collective                          bytes    status
  "ep"    lhs{R->S0}    rhs{S0}       -> S0         local_slice(lhs)                      1.25   chosen, reshards lhs
          lhs{R}        rhs{S0->R}    -> R          allgather(rhs)                       15360
          lhs{R}        rhs{S0->S2}   -> S2         all_to_all(rhs)                      15360
          lhs{R->S1}    rhs{S0->S1}   -> S1         local_slice(lhs), all_to_all(rhs)   15361.2
          lhs{R->P}     rhs{S0->R}    -> P          keep_one_copy(lhs), allgather(rhs)  15361.2  not allowed
  result  lhs{"ep":S0}  rhs{"ep":S0}  -> {"ep":S0}

  lhs = transfer_to(lhs, DeviceMapping(mesh, (Sharded(0),)))
  with auto_reshard(mode="silent"):
  at /var/folders/gg/plf_qcqs5556rt10q1s96gl40000gn/T/ipykernel_89305/3728650120.py:45 in forward()
max difference from the reference: 2.9e-06

The output matches. MAX resharded the routing weights to match the experts’ layout, so each device multiplies only its own experts’ slice, and the weighted outputs come out as partial sums that to_numpy() all-reduces.

This dense form sends every token to every device. Expert-parallel serving sends each token only to the devices that hold its chosen experts, an all-to-all exchange, and returns the results with a second one. With many experts this moves far less data, but the communication is uneven and depends on the routing. MAX serves MoE models such as GPT-OSS and DeepSeek, and supports expert parallelism across GPUs with all-to-all dispatch and combine steps.

Exercises

  1. Increase top_k to 8 (every expert). Does the output equal a weighted sum of 8 dense MLPs? What would the model lose?
  2. Implement the routed version for one device: for each expert, gather its tokens, run the MLP on them only, and scatter the results back. Check it against MoE.
  3. Make the router unfair by scaling one row of gate.weight by 5. How does the load per expert change, and what would that do to expert-parallel serving?

What you built

Chapter 6 extended your model with three features of current open models: normalized queries and keys, sliding-window attention, and mixtures of experts. Chapter 7 serves your own architecture with max serve, benchmarks it, and constrains its output.

7.1 Serve your architecture

In Section 1.1 you served SmolLM2 with MAX’s own implementation. This section serves yours: the LlamaForCausalLM you wrote in Section 1.6, behind the same AutoPipeline API and the same OpenAI-compatible server. MAX loads model code from outside its own package through custom architectures: a Python package that describes the model, which max serve imports with --custom-architectures.

PROMPT = ["The capital of France is"]

First, the reference: SmolLM2 as MAX implements it, loaded with AutoPipeline.

reference = AutoPipeline(MODEL_ID, quantization_encoding="float32")
expected = reference(PROMPT, max_new_tokens=16, top_k=1)

['The capital of France is Paris. Paris is the largest city in France and the capital of the French department']

A custom architecture package

A MAX architecture has three parts, all in the package below:

  • A config class tells MAX the sizes it needs to plan memory before it loads anything: the number of layers, key/value heads, and head size. It also declares the supported encodings; this model computes in float32.
  • A pipeline model loads the weights and runs the model. MAX gives it the checkpoint’s weights and, at each step, the request’s tokens, and it returns the next-token logits. Here it builds your LlamaForCausalLM with load_model from Section 1.6, casts the checkpoint’s bfloat16 weights to float32, and compiles it.
  • A SupportedArchitecture ties them together under the name in the checkpoint’s config.json, here LlamaForCausalLM, along with the tokenizer and the weight format. ARCHITECTURES lists it for --custom-architectures to find.

To keep the package short, this pipeline model reruns the whole sequence at each step and serves one request at a time (max_batch_size is 1), like the generation loop in Section 1.7. MAX’s own architectures use its paged KV cache and continuous batching instead: they implement a decoder module that reads keys and values from the cache. MAX’s OLMo 3 architecture is a compact example.

from __future__ import annotations

from collections.abc import Sequence
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any, ClassVar

import numpy as np
from max.driver import Buffer, Device
from max.dtype import DType
from max.engine import InferenceSession
from max.graph import DeviceRef
from max.graph.weights import Weights, WeightsAdapter, WeightsFormat
from max.nn.kv_cache import KVCacheInputs, KVCacheParams
from max.nn.outputs import ReturnHiddenStates, ReturnLogits
from max.pipelines.context import TextContext
from max.pipelines.lib import (
    KVCacheConfig,
    MAXModelConfig,
    MemoryPlan,
    ModelInputs,
    ModelOutputs,
    PipelineConfig,
    PipelineModelWithKVCache,
    SupportedArchitecture,
    SupportedEncoding,
    TextTokenizer,
)
from max.pipelines.lib.interfaces.arch_config import (
    ArchConfigWithAttentionKVCache,
)
from max.pipelines.lib.utils import (
    parse_state_dict_from_weights,
    upper_bounded_default,
)
from max.pipelines.modeling.types import PipelineTask
from max.tensor import Tensor
from max.tensor.tensor import TensorType
from transformers import AutoConfig

from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import load_model


@dataclass
class MinillmArchConfig(ArchConfigWithAttentionKVCache):
    DEFAULT_ENCODING: ClassVar[SupportedEncoding] = "float32"
    SUPPORTED_ENCODINGS: ClassVar[set[SupportedEncoding]] = {"float32"}

    @property
    def num_key_value_heads(self) -> int:
        return self.huggingface_config.num_key_value_heads

    @property
    def head_dim(self) -> int:
        hf = self.huggingface_config
        return (
            getattr(hf, "head_dim", None)
            or hf.hidden_size // hf.num_attention_heads
        )

    @property
    def num_layers(self) -> int:
        return self.huggingface_config.num_hidden_layers

    @classmethod
    def calculate_max_seq_len(
        cls, huggingface_config: AutoConfig, model_config: MAXModelConfig
    ) -> int:
        return upper_bounded_default(
            upper_bound=huggingface_config.max_position_embeddings,
            default=model_config.max_length,
        )


@dataclass
class TokenInputs(ModelInputs):
    tokens: Buffer

    @property
    def buffers(self) -> tuple[Buffer, ...]:
        return (self.tokens,)


class MinillmPipelineModel(PipelineModelWithKVCache[TextContext]):
    def __init__(
        self,
        pipeline_config: PipelineConfig,
        session: InferenceSession,
        devices: list[Device],
        kv_cache_config: KVCacheConfig,
        weights: Weights,
        adapter: WeightsAdapter | None,
        return_logits: ReturnLogits,
        *,
        memory_plan: MemoryPlan,
        return_hidden_states: ReturnHiddenStates = ReturnHiddenStates.NONE,
        max_batch_size: int = 1,
    ) -> None:
        super().__init__(
            pipeline_config=pipeline_config,
            session=session,
            devices=devices,
            kv_cache_config=kv_cache_config,
            weights=weights,
            adapter=adapter,
            return_logits=return_logits,
            return_hidden_states=return_hidden_states,
            max_batch_size=max_batch_size,
            memory_plan=memory_plan,
        )
        hf = self.huggingface_config
        config = LlamaConfig(
            **{
                k: getattr(hf, k)
                for k in LlamaConfig.__dataclass_fields__
                if hasattr(hf, k)
            }
        )
        state = parse_state_dict_from_weights(
            self.pipeline_config, weights, adapter
        )
        state = {name: w.astype(DType.float32) for name, w in state.items()}
        model = load_model(config, state, self.devices[0])
        self.model = model.compile(
            TensorType(
                DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=self.devices[0]
            )
        )

    @classmethod
    def get_kv_params(
        cls,
        huggingface_config: Any,
        pipeline_config: PipelineConfig,
        devices: list[DeviceRef],
        kv_cache_config: KVCacheConfig,
        cache_dtype: DType,
    ) -> KVCacheParams:
        hf = huggingface_config
        return kv_cache_config.to_params(
            dtype=cache_dtype,
            n_kv_heads=hf.num_key_value_heads,
            head_dim=getattr(hf, "head_dim", None)
            or hf.hidden_size // hf.num_attention_heads,
            num_layers=hf.num_hidden_layers,
            devices=devices,
            data_parallel_degree=pipeline_config.model.data_parallel_degree,
        )

    def execute(self, model_inputs: ModelInputs) -> ModelOutputs:
        assert isinstance(model_inputs, TokenInputs)
        logits = self.model(Tensor.from_dlpack(model_inputs.tokens))
        last = Buffer.from_numpy(
            np.ascontiguousarray(logits.to_numpy()[0, -1:, :])
        ).to(self.devices[0])
        return ModelOutputs(logits=last, next_token_logits=last)

    def prepare_initial_token_inputs(
        self,
        replica_batches: Sequence[Sequence[TextContext]],
        kv_cache_inputs: KVCacheInputs[Buffer, Buffer] | None = None,
        return_n_logits: int = 1,
    ) -> TokenInputs:
        ids = replica_batches[0][0].tokens.all
        inputs = TokenInputs(
            tokens=Buffer.from_numpy(np.array([ids], dtype=np.int64)).to(
                self.devices[0]
            )
        )
        inputs.kv_cache_inputs = kv_cache_inputs
        return inputs


def _identity_adapter(state_dict: dict[str, Weights], **_: object) -> dict:
    return {name: w.data() for name, w in state_dict.items()}


minillm_arch = SupportedArchitecture(
    name="LlamaForCausalLM",
    task=PipelineTask.TEXT_GENERATION,
    example_repo_ids=["HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M-Instruct"],
    default_weights_format=WeightsFormat.safetensors,
    default_encoding=MinillmArchConfig.DEFAULT_ENCODING,
    supported_encodings=MinillmArchConfig.SUPPORTED_ENCODINGS,
    pipeline_model=MinillmPipelineModel,
    tokenizer=TextTokenizer,
    context_type=TextContext,
    multi_gpu_supported=False,
    weight_adapters={WeightsFormat.safetensors: _identity_adapter},
    config=MinillmArchConfig,
    required_arguments={"enable_prefix_caching": False, "max_batch_size": 1},
    supports_overlap_scheduler=False,
    supports_device_graph_capture=False,
)
ARCHITECTURES = [minillm_arch]

Saved to minillm/serve/__init__.py.

Use it in Python

import_custom_architectures registers the package’s architectures, as --custom-architectures does for the CLI. A registered architecture with the same name as one of MAX’s replaces it in this process. When an architecture has a newer module-based implementation, MAX prefers that one, so prefer_module_v3=False selects the registered LlamaForCausalLM:

import_custom_architectures(["minillm.serve"])
ours = AutoPipeline(
    MODEL_ID, quantization_encoding="float32", prefer_module_v3=False
)
print("pipeline model:", type(ours.model.pipeline._pipeline_model).__name__)
result = ours(PROMPT, max_new_tokens=16, top_k=1)
ours.close()
print(result)
print("same as MAX's implementation:", result == expected)
assert result == expected

pipeline model: MinillmPipelineModel
['The capital of France is Paris. Paris is the largest city in France and the capital of the French department']
same as MAX's implementation: True

The pipeline model is yours, and its greedy output matches MAX’s implementation token for token.

Serve it

The same package serves over HTTP. --custom-architectures minillm.serve imports it, and --no-prefer-module-v3 selects it over MAX’s own implementation:

server = subprocess.Popen(
    [
        "max",
        "serve",
        "--model",
        MODEL_ID,
        "--port",
        "8000",
        "--quantization-encoding",
        "float32",
        "--custom-architectures",
        "minillm.serve",
        "--no-prefer-module-v3",
    ],
    stdout=subprocess.DEVNULL,
    stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL,
)
start = time.perf_counter()
while True:
    try:
        urllib.request.urlopen("http://localhost:8000/health")
        break
    except OSError:
        if server.poll() is not None:
            raise RuntimeError(
                "max serve exited; run it in a terminal to see why"
            )
        time.sleep(1)
print(f"server ready in {time.perf_counter() - start:.0f} s")

client = OpenAI(base_url="http://localhost:8000/v1", api_key="unused")
completion = client.completions.create(
    model=MODEL_ID, prompt=PROMPT[0], max_tokens=16, temperature=0
)
print(PROMPT[0] + completion.choices[0].text)
assert PROMPT[0] + completion.choices[0].text == expected[0]
server.terminate()
_ = server.wait()

server ready in 14 s
The capital of France is Paris. Paris is the largest city in France and the capital of the French department

Your model now answers an OpenAI client exactly as MAX’s implementation does. The handbook’s OpenAI-compatible API page covers the request format. MAX’s guide to serving custom model architectures covers the production version: a decoder module on MAX’s paged KV cache, with batching and GPU kernels.

Section 7.2 measures the server under load with max benchmark.

7.2 Benchmark a server

A server that answers one request correctly can still fall over under real traffic. max benchmark measures a running server the way users experience it: it sends many requests, some at the same time, and records how long each one waits for its first token and for each token after that. This section benchmarks MAX’s SmolLM2 and the architecture you served in Section 7.1, and explains the numbers.

import json
import subprocess
import tempfile
import time
import urllib.request
from collections.abc import Iterator
from contextlib import contextmanager
from pathlib import Path

import plotly.graph_objects as go
from plotly.subplots import make_subplots

from minillm import viz
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID

WORKDIR = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp())

What to measure

Four numbers describe a serving run. The handbook’s LLM inference metrics page defines them in detail:

  • Time to first token (TTFT): the time from sending a request to receiving its first token. It includes the wait in the queue and the prefill. Users notice it as the delay before the answer starts.
  • Time per output token (TPOT): the average gap between later tokens, one decode step. Users notice it as the reading speed.
  • Requests per second and output tokens per second: the throughput of the whole server, which decides how many users one GPU can serve.

Concurrency, the number of requests in flight at once, connects them. More concurrency lets the server batch more requests per step (Chapter 5), which raises throughput, but each request can then wait longer.

Run a benchmark

From a terminal, a benchmark is one command against a running server. This one sends 32 requests of 128 random input tokens each, asks for 32 output tokens per request, and keeps at most 8 in flight:

max benchmark --model HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M-Instruct --dataset-name random \
  --random-input-len 128 --random-output-len 32 \
  --num-prompts 32 --max-concurrency 8 --result-filename result.json

The random dataset gives every run the same shape, which suits comparisons. The default dataset, sharegpt, replays real chat conversations with varied lengths. The two helpers below start a server and run a benchmark from Python, then read the result file:

@contextmanager
def server(*flags: str) -> Iterator[None]:
    process = subprocess.Popen(
        [
            "max",
            "serve",
            "--model",
            MODEL_ID,
            "--port",
            "8000",
            "--quantization-encoding",
            "float32",
            *flags,
        ],
        stdout=subprocess.DEVNULL,
        stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL,
    )
    try:
        while True:
            try:
                urllib.request.urlopen("http://localhost:8000/health")
                break
            except OSError:
                if process.poll() is not None:
                    raise RuntimeError(
                        "max serve exited; run it in a terminal to see why"
                    )
                time.sleep(1)
        yield
    finally:
        process.terminate()
        process.wait()


def benchmark(concurrency: int, prompts: int) -> dict[str, float]:
    result = WORKDIR / f"result_{concurrency}.json"
    subprocess.run(
        [
            "max",
            "benchmark",
            "--model",
            MODEL_ID,
            "--port",
            "8000",
            "--dataset-name",
            "random",
            "--random-input-len",
            "128",
            "--random-output-len",
            "32",
            "--num-prompts",
            str(prompts),
            "--max-concurrency",
            str(concurrency),
            "--result-filename",
            str(result),
            "--log-dir",
            str(WORKDIR / "logs"),
        ],
        check=True,
        stdout=subprocess.DEVNULL,
        stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL,
    )
    run = json.loads(result.read_text())
    return {
        "concurrency": concurrency,
        "requests/s": run["request_throughput"],
        "output tokens/s": run["total_output_tokens"] / run["duration"],
        "TTFT (ms)": run["mean_ttft_ms"],
        "TPOT (ms)": run["mean_tpot_ms"],
    }


def show(rows: list[dict[str, float]]) -> None:
    print("".join(f"{name:>17}" for name in rows[0]))
    for row in rows:
        print(
            "".join(
                f"{value:>17.1f}"
                if isinstance(value, float)
                else f"{value:>17}"
                for value in row.values()
            )
        )

At concurrency 8, max benchmark leaves the first and last 8 requests out of its statistics, because the server is still filling up or draining while they run. The runs at concurrency 8 therefore send more requests.

MAX’s SmolLM2

with server():
    max_runs = [benchmark(1, prompts=16), benchmark(8, prompts=32)]
show(max_runs)

      concurrency       requests/s  output tokens/s        TTFT (ms)        TPOT (ms)
                1              1.4             45.2             80.7             20.2
                8              8.6            275.6            275.2             21.1

With 8 requests in flight, the server batches them into each decode step. Throughput rises several times, while TPOT barely changes: one decode step costs about the same for 8 sequences as for 1. At this model size, most of a step’s cost is fixed work, such as reading the weights and launching kernels, and little of it is arithmetic per sequence. The handbook’s batching page explains why. TTFT rises because requests that arrive together are prefilled together, and a new request waits for the current step to finish.

Your architecture

Now run the same benchmark against the package from Section 7.1:

with server("--custom-architectures", "minillm.serve", "--no-prefer-module-v3"):
    ours = [
        benchmark(1, prompts=8),
        benchmark(8, prompts=24),
    ]  # fewer: this server is slow
show(ours)

      concurrency       requests/s  output tokens/s        TTFT (ms)        TPOT (ms)
                1              0.5             14.4             73.5             69.1
                8              0.2              6.3          18269.0             96.8

max_gain = max_runs[1]["requests/s"] / max_runs[0]["requests/s"]
ours_gain = ours[1]["requests/s"] / ours[0]["requests/s"]
print(
    f"throughput gain from concurrency 8: MAX's {max_gain:.1f}x, yours {ours_gain:.1f}x"
)
assert max_gain > 1.5 and ours_gain < 1.5

metrics = ["requests/s", "output tokens/s", "TTFT (ms)", "TPOT (ms)"]
fig = make_subplots(
    rows=1, cols=4, horizontal_spacing=0.07, subplot_titles=metrics
)
for name, rows, color in [
    ("MAX's SmolLM2", max_runs, viz.BLUE),
    ("yours (Section 7.1)", ours, viz.ORANGE),
]:
    for col, metric in enumerate(metrics, start=1):
        fig.add_trace(
            go.Bar(
                x=[f"c = {r['concurrency']}" for r in rows],
                y=[r[metric] for r in rows],
                name=name,
                marker_color=color,
                legendgroup=name,
                showlegend=col == 1,
                hovertemplate=f"{name}, %{{x}}: %{{y:,.1f}} {metric}<extra></extra>",
            ),
            row=1,
            col=col,
        )
fig.update_layout(
    title="Serving SmolLM2 at concurrency 1 and 8",
    barmode="group",
    height=440,
    legend=dict(y=-0.12, yanchor="top"),
    margin=dict(t=96),
)
viz.show(fig)

throughput gain from concurrency 8: MAX's 6.1x, yours 0.4x

The two servers differ in two ways, and earlier sections explain both:

  • TPOT is several times higher. The pipeline model from Section 7.1 reruns the whole sequence for every token, as Section 1.7 did, so a step grows with the sequence. The KV cache from Section 1.8 makes it constant.
  • Concurrency doesn’t raise throughput. The package declares max_batch_size 1, so the server runs one request at a time and queues the rest. The queue shows up in TTFT: a request waits for the ones ahead of it to finish completely.

The engine in Chapter 5 fixed both with a paged KV cache and continuous batching. MAX’s own architectures get them from MAX’s serving stack, which is why a production architecture implements a decoder on MAX’s paged KV cache (Section 7.1 links the guide).

Reading benchmarks well

  • Benchmark the workload you will serve. Input and output lengths change the results a lot: long prompts stress prefill and TTFT, and long answers stress decode and TPOT.
  • Sweep concurrency, for example --max-concurrency 1,4,16,64, and plot throughput against TTFT or TPOT. Throughput levels off once the GPU is busy, while latency keeps rising. The useful operating point is just before throughput levels off.
  • Set a latency target first, such as a TTFT under 500 ms at the 95th percentile, then find the highest throughput that meets it. The result file stores percentiles (p95_ttft_ms, p99_tpot_ms, and so on) next to the means.

The handbook’s LLM performance benchmarks page covers benchmark design, and max benchmark --help lists the other datasets and options.

Exercises

  1. Sweep the concurrency of MAX’s SmolLM2 over 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, and 32. Where does throughput stop growing, and what happens to TTFT after that?
  2. Repeat the MAX run with --random-input-len 1024. Which metric changes most, and why?
  3. Do the second exercise of Section 7.1, raise max_batch_size in its package to 4, and benchmark it again at concurrency 8.

Next

Section 7.3 constrains what the server generates: JSON that follows a schema, and calls to your own functions.

7.3 Structured output and tools

A chat answer is for people. A program needs output it can parse: a JSON object with the right fields, or the name of a function to call with its arguments. Instructions in the prompt don’t get that reliably, least of all from a small model. This section makes the server guarantee the format with structured output, then builds function calling on top of it.

import json
import subprocess
import time
import urllib.request

from openai import OpenAI
from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field

from minillm.config import MODEL_ID

Structured output is off by default, and --enable-structured-output turns it on:

server = subprocess.Popen(
    [
        "max",
        "serve",
        "--model",
        MODEL_ID,
        "--port",
        "8000",
        "--quantization-encoding",
        "float32",
        "--enable-structured-output",
    ],
    stdout=subprocess.DEVNULL,
    stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL,
)
while True:
    try:
        urllib.request.urlopen("http://localhost:8000/health")
        break
    except OSError:
        if server.poll() is not None:
            raise RuntimeError(
                "max serve exited; run it in a terminal to see why"
            )
        time.sleep(1)
client = OpenAI(base_url="http://localhost:8000/v1", api_key="unused")


def chat(messages: list[dict[str, str]], **options: object) -> str:
    completion = client.chat.completions.create(
        model=MODEL_ID,
        messages=messages,
        max_tokens=100,
        temperature=0,
        **options,
    )
    return completion.choices[0].message.content

JSON that follows a schema

Without a constraint, the model answers in prose:

question = [
    {
        "role": "user",
        "content": "Tell me about Rome: its country and three landmarks.",
    }
]
print(chat(question))

Rome, the Eternal City, is a city that has witnessed so much history and transformation. As a helpful AI assistant, I'm here to share with you the fascinating facts about Rome, its country, and three iconic landmarks.

Rome, the Eternal City, is a city that has witnessed so much history and transformation. It's a place where ancient civilizations like the Romans, Greeks, and Etruscans have left their mark, and it's a place where the Romans have built a lasting

Describe the answer you want as a Pydantic model instead. Pydantic turns it into a JSON schema, which the request passes as response_format:

class City(BaseModel):
    model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")  # no fields beyond these
    name: str
    country: str
    landmarks: list[str] = Field(min_length=3, max_length=3)


schema = {
    "type": "json_schema",
    "json_schema": {"name": "City", "schema": City.model_json_schema()},
}
answer = chat(question, response_format=schema)
print(answer)
city = City.model_validate_json(answer)
city

{"name":"Rome","country":"Italy","landmarks":["The Colosseum","The Roman Forum","The Pantheon"]}
City(name='Rome', country='Italy', landmarks=['The Colosseum', 'The Roman Forum', 'The Pantheon'])

model_validate_json raises if the text doesn’t match the schema. With structured output, the text always matches.

How the server guarantees it

The guarantee comes from the sampler. MAX compiles the schema into a grammar, and at every decode step the grammar says which tokens may come next: after {"name": "Rome", , only tokens that continue toward "country". MAX masks every other token out of the logits before sampling, as the top-p filter in Section 1.7 did with \(-\infty\), so the model can only pick a token that keeps the output valid. Generation ends when the grammar is complete.

The schema fixes the form of the answer and leaves the facts to the model. The model can skip any requirement that the schema leaves out: without min_length, a small model may return an empty landmarks list. Put every requirement you can into the schema, and check the values that matter. The handbook’s structured outputs page covers schema design and the cost of constrained decoding.

Function calling

A tool is a function that the model may ask your program to run: look up the weather, query a database, call an API. The model never runs anything itself. It writes the function’s name and arguments, your code runs the function, and the result goes back into the conversation. A tool call is therefore a structured output whose schema is “one of these functions, with valid arguments for it”.

Here are two tools, each a Python function plus a schema for its arguments:

RATES = {"USD": 1.0, "EUR": 0.92, "JPY": 151.0}


def get_weather(city: str) -> dict[str, object]:
    return {
        "city": city,
        "temperature_c": 18,
        "sky": "light rain",
    }  # a stand-in for a weather API


def convert_currency(
    amount: float, source: str, target: str
) -> dict[str, object]:
    return {
        "amount": round(amount / RATES[source] * RATES[target], 2),
        "currency": target,
    }


def arguments(**properties: dict[str, object]) -> dict[str, object]:
    return {
        "type": "object",
        "properties": properties,
        "required": list(properties),
        "additionalProperties": False,
    }


TOOLS = {
    "get_weather": (
        get_weather,
        "Gets the current weather in a city.",
        arguments(city={"type": "string"}),
    ),
    "convert_currency": (
        convert_currency,
        "Converts an amount between currencies.",
        arguments(
            amount={"type": "number"},
            source={"enum": list(RATES)},
            target={"enum": list(RATES)},
        ),
    ),
}

The schema for a call allows any one of the tools: anyOf lists one object per tool, each with its fixed name and its own arguments. enum limits the currencies to the ones RATES knows, so the model can’t ask for one the function would fail on. The system prompt tells the model what the tools do:

call_schema = {
    "anyOf": [
        {
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {"name": {"const": name}, "arguments": args},
            "required": ["name", "arguments"],
            "additionalProperties": False,
        }
        for name, (_, _, args) in TOOLS.items()
    ]
}
system = (
    "You can call these functions. Reply with one call as JSON.\n"
    + "\n".join(
        f"- {name}: {description} Arguments: {json.dumps(args['properties'])}"
        for name, (_, description, args) in TOOLS.items()
    )
)


def call_tool(question: str) -> tuple[dict[str, object], dict[str, object]]:
    messages = [
        {"role": "system", "content": system},
        {"role": "user", "content": question},
    ]
    call = json.loads(
        chat(
            messages,
            response_format={
                "type": "json_schema",
                "json_schema": {"name": "call", "schema": call_schema},
            },
        )
    )
    function = TOOLS[call["name"]][0]
    return call, function(**call["arguments"])


for question in [
    "What's the weather in Tokyo?",
    "How much is 20 US dollars in euros?",
]:
    call, result = call_tool(question)
    print(f"{question}\n  call:   {call}\n  result: {result}")

What's the weather in Tokyo?
  call:   {'name': 'get_weather', 'arguments': {'city': 'Tokyo'}}
  result: {'city': 'Tokyo', 'temperature_c': 18, 'sky': 'light rain'}
How much is 20 US dollars in euros?
  call:   {'name': 'convert_currency', 'arguments': {'amount': 20, 'source': 'USD', 'target': 'EUR'}}
  result: {'amount': 18.4, 'currency': 'EUR'}

Every call parses and runs, since the grammar allows nothing else. Whether the model picks the right tool is up to the model; this one does on these questions.

The last step gives the result back so the model can answer. Here the result goes into the system prompt:

question = "Should I take an umbrella in Paris today?"
call, result = call_tool(question)
print("call:", call)
print(
    chat(
        [
            {
                "role": "system",
                "content": f"Current weather: {json.dumps(result)}",
            },
            {"role": "user", "content": question},
        ]
    )
)

call: {'name': 'get_weather', 'arguments': {'city': 'Paris'}}
As a traveler, it's essential to consider the weather forecast and potential weather-related issues before making a decision. Paris is a city known for its rain, and the city's weather can be unpredictable.

If you're planning to visit Paris in the morning, it's best to take an umbrella. The city's rain can be heavy and unpredictable, especially in the mornings. A rain umbrella can provide some protection from the rain, but it's not a guarantee.

If you're

server.terminate()
_ = server.wait()

A 135M-parameter model uses the result loosely. Larger models follow it much more closely, especially ones trained for function calling.

Those models also take tools through the tools parameter of the OpenAI API. Their chat templates describe the tools to the model in the format it was trained on, and the model writes calls in its own format. MAX parses those calls with a tool parser for each model family, such as Llama, Qwen, and DeepSeek, and returns them as message.tool_calls; tool_choice="required" forces a call. SmolLM2’s chat template has no place for tools, which is why this section built the protocol by hand. The mechanism is the same in both cases: the model writes a structured call, and your code runs it. The handbook’s function calling page covers the API and the agent loops built on it.

Exercises

  1. Remove Field(min_length=3, max_length=3) from City. What does the model return now?
  2. Add a third tool, get_time(city), and a question that needs it. Does the model choose it?
  3. max benchmark takes --response-format with a JSON schema. Benchmark the server with and without one, as in Section 7.2. How much does constrained decoding cost per token?

Next

Section 7.4 points the same client at state-of-the-art open models on Modular’s cloud.

7.4 Modular’s cloud

Everything in this book ran on your own machine: models of up to 0.6B parameters, with multi-device meshes simulated on a CPU. Serving state-of-the-art open models to real traffic takes more: the largest models span many GPUs, capacity has to follow demand, and someone has to respond when a node fails. Modular’s cloud is an inference service that runs those models on MAX, the framework you used in this book. This last section points your client code at it by changing the base URL, the API key, and the model name.

import os
import subprocess
import time
import urllib.request

from openai import OpenAI

from minillm.config import MODEL_ID

Two ways to call a hosted model

Modular’s cloud serves popular open models behind the same OpenAI-compatible API that the earlier sections of this chapter used:

  • Shared endpoints are billed per token, and their capacity scales automatically, down to zero when idle.
  • Dedicated endpoints reserve GPUs for you and are billed per minute, for consistent latency and guaranteed throughput.

The pricing page compares them.

The same client, a different URL

Every server in Chapter 7 spoke the OpenAI API, and so do Modular’s endpoints. Here is the client from Section 7.1 against a local server again:

server = subprocess.Popen(
    [
        "max",
        "serve",
        "--model",
        MODEL_ID,
        "--port",
        "8000",
        "--quantization-encoding",
        "float32",
    ],
    stdout=subprocess.DEVNULL,
    stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL,
)
while True:
    try:
        urllib.request.urlopen("http://localhost:8000/health")
        break
    except OSError:
        if server.poll() is not None:
            raise RuntimeError(
                "max serve exited; run it in a terminal to see why"
            )
        time.sleep(1)


def ask(client: OpenAI, model: str, question: str) -> str:
    completion = client.chat.completions.create(
        model=model,
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": question}],
        max_tokens=60,
    )
    return completion.choices[0].message.content


local = OpenAI(base_url="http://localhost:8000/v1", api_key="unused")
print(ask(local, MODEL_ID, "What is the capital of France?"))
server.terminate()
_ = server.wait()

The capital city of France is Paris. It is located in the northern part of the country, in the eastern part of the Po River, and is the cultural heart of France. Paris is known for its rich history, iconic landmarks, and stunning architecture.

To call a hosted model instead:

  1. Sign up at console.modular.com.
  2. Create an API key with model access on the API keys page, and set it as the MODULAR_API_KEY environment variable. In Colab, add it as a secret and copy it into os.environ.
  3. Pick a model on the endpoints page, and set its model ID as MODULAR_MODEL.

Only the base URL, the key, and the model name change. The cell skips the request when no key is set:

api_key = os.environ.get("MODULAR_API_KEY")
if api_key is None:
    print("Set MODULAR_API_KEY and MODULAR_MODEL to call a hosted model.")
else:
    hosted = OpenAI(base_url="https://api.modular.com/v1", api_key=api_key)
    print(
        ask(
            hosted,
            os.environ["MODULAR_MODEL"],
            "What is the capital of France?",
        )
    )

Set MODULAR_API_KEY and MODULAR_MODEL to call a hosted model.

The API is the same, so code written against it, such as the structured output in Section 7.3, works with hosted models that support the feature. max benchmark measures a hosted endpoint as it measured your local server. The mcloud backend reads the key from OPENAI_API_KEY, and because a hosted endpoint manages its own cache, the command skips flushing it:

OPENAI_API_KEY=$MODULAR_API_KEY max benchmark --backend mcloud \
  --base-url https://api.modular.com --endpoint /v1/chat/completions \
  --no-flush-prefix-cache --model $MODULAR_MODEL \
  --dataset-name random --random-input-len 128 --random-output-len 32 \
  --num-prompts 32 --max-concurrency 8

Before you choose between shared and dedicated endpoints, benchmark with your real prompt and answer lengths; the latency targets from Section 7.2 apply unchanged. The handbook’s choosing the right model and calculating GPU memory pages help you choose a model and estimate the GPUs it needs.

What you built

You started this book with three lines of AutoPipeline. Since then you:

  • wrote SmolLM2 from tensors up, checked it against MAX’s implementation, and added sampling and a KV cache (Chapter 1);
  • sharded it across a device mesh (Chapter 2);
  • wrote a Mojo kernel and built a graph by hand (Chapter 3);
  • tested, benchmarked, and profiled it (Chapter 4);
  • built a serving engine with continuous batching, a paged KV cache, prefix caching, and speculative decoding (Chapter 5);
  • brought up Qwen3, Gemma 3, and a mixture of experts (Chapter 6);
  • served your own architecture, measured it, and constrained its output (Chapter 7).

A model takes the same path into MAX, at a larger size. To keep going:

  • Read MAX’s guide to model bring-up and port a model you care about.
  • Work through the Mojo GPU puzzles to write the kernels behind it.
  • Read the handbook for the ideas behind every chapter.
  • Sign up to run state-of-the-art open models on Modular’s cloud, or request a demo to talk about your workload.
